The Sample Complexity of Membership Inference and Privacy Auditing
Mahdi Haghifam, Adam Smith, Jonathan Ullman

Abstract
A membership-inference attack gets the output of a learning algorithm, and a target individual, and tries to determine whether this individual is a member of the training data or an independent sample from the same distribution. A successful membership-inference attack typically requires the attacker to have some knowledge about the distribution that the training data was sampled from, and this knowledge is often captured through a set of independent reference samples from that distribution. In this work we study how much information the attacker needs for membership inference by investigating the sample complexity—the minimum number of reference samples required—for a successful attack. We study this question in the fundamental setting of Gaussian mean estimation where the learning algorithm is given n n samples from a Gaussian distribution 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ , Σ ) \mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma) in d d dimensions, and tries to estimate μ ^ \hat{\mu} up to some error 𝔼 ⁡ [ ‖ μ ^ − μ ‖ Σ 2 ] ≤ ρ 2 ​ d \mathbb{E}[\|\hat{\mu}-\mu\|^{2}_{\Sigma}]\leq\rho^{2}d . Our result shows that for membership inference in this setting, Ω ⁡ ( n + n 2 ​ ρ 2 ) \Omega(n+n^{2}\rho^{2}) samples can be necessary to carry out any attack that competes with a fully informed attacker. Our result is the first to show that the attacker sometimes needs many more samples than the training algorithm uses to train the model. This result has significant implications for practice, as all attacks used in practice have a restricted form that uses O ⁡ ( n ) O(n) samples and cannot benefit from ω ⁡ ( n ) \omega(n) samples. Thus, these attacks may be underestimating the possibility of membership inference, and better attacks may be possible when information about the distribution is easy to obtain.



1
Introduction

The objective of machine learning is to discover statistical patterns in a population using training data sampled from that population. Intuitively, an ideal learning algorithm would extract only information about the population itself and nothing particular to the specific training data, so it would ensure the privacy of the people who are represented by the training data. However, real learning algorithms necessarily have some dependence on their training data, and there are many powerful privacy attacks that can use the output of a learning algorithm to make inferences about the training data, including reconstruction attacks
[
DN03
]
, data extraction attacks
[
CLEKS19
,
Car+21
]
, and
membership-inference attacks

[
Hom+08
,
SOJH09
,
DSSUV15
,
SSSS17
]
, which are the subject of this work.

A privacy attack is most compelling if it can be carried out by a realistic attacker in a realistic environment.
What makes the attacker and environment realistic is complex, and depends on many features, such as the type of populations, learning objectives, and training algorithms involved. One of the most important features of an attack is
the attacker’s background knowledge about the population.

Our goal in this work is to understand how little an attacker needs to know
about the population to mount a successful attack.

Membership-inference attacks.

To make this question concrete, we study an important class of privacy attacks called
membership-inference attacks (MIA)
. Informally, a membership-inference attack gets the output of a learning algorithm, and a target individual that is either a sample from the training data itself or an independent sample from the same population as the training data, and tries to distinguish between the two cases. A successful attack can be a serious violation of privacy on its own, since membership in the dataset itself can be highly sensitive (e.g. if the dataset represents a study on patients with a rare disease) or it can be a stepping stone to other kinds of privacy violations where the attacker learns some features of the targeted individual.

Membership-inference attacks have been influential in both the theory and practice of privacy for a long time. They were first demonstrated in practice on genomic datasets by
[
Hom+08
]
, whose attack was formally studied by
[
SOJH09
]
. In parallel, membership-inference attacks were introduced by as a way to prove tight lower bounds on the error of differentially private algorithms
[
BUV14
]
. These two perspectives on membership-inference were unified by
[
DSSUV15
]
, who showed how the lower bounds for differential privacy yield membership-inference attacks with very strong robustness properties.
Building on the connection of MIA and lower bounds for differential privacy for the mean estimation task, a long line of work has leveraged MIAs to establish sharp privacy–utility trade-offs for various estimation and learning tasks (e.g.,
[
BST14
,
SU17
,
KMS22
,
CWZ23
,
Nar24
,
PH25
]
).
Later on, the influential work of
[
SSSS17
]
showed how to perform membership-inference on black-box neural networks. Membership-inference attacks were established by
[
JUO20
]
as a widely adopted method for empirically auditing the privacy of machine learning algorithms in practice
(see also
[
SNJ23
,
MMC24
,
KSL25
]
for recent developments.). Membership-inference attacks have also found important applications in studying adaptive data analysis
[
HU14
,
SU15
]
and memorization
[
ADHLR24
]
in machine learning.

In both the theoretical and empirical research on membership-inference, the attacker needs fairly precise knowledge of the population in order to pull off the most successful attacks.
The primary goal of this work is to understand whether this precise knowledge is
necessary
to implement successful attacks. In this work we make this question precise using the language of
sample complexity
—we assume that the attacker is given a set of auxiliary samples from the same population as the training data, and want to know:

how many samples are necessary and sufficient to implement a successful membership-inference attack?

Known attacks use

O(n)
reference examples.

All known membership-inference attacks can be implemented using sample access to the underlying population; furthermore, they use
O(n)
such samples, where
n
is the size of the training data. For example, empirical work on MIA (e.g.
[
SSSS17
,
YGFJ18
,
JUO20
,
YMMBS22
,
SNJ23
,
MMC24
,
ZLS24
]
) generally considers attacks that use the reference examples to train a small set of
shadow
or
reference
models that are distributed identically to the real model. Each such model uses only
n
reference examples; moreover they are often trained on overlapping samples, which limits the reference data to just
2n
examples
[
Car+22
]
). These attacks’ specific form uses the shadow models only to set the tests’ threshold, and does not benefit benefit from having
\omega(n)
samples.

A few works
[
CG24
,
SZE24
]
use a quadratic approximation to the training loss to design a membership test, instead of comparing to reference models. These works do not directly quantify the size of the reference sample they use because they consider a threat model in which the attacker can query the Hessian on the training set.

On the theoretical side,
[
SOJH09
]
analyzed a simple setting in which
O(n)
reference samples are sufficient to carry out an optimal attack.
[
DSSUV15
]
gave a robust version of that attack that also uses
O(n)
samples—in fact, when the trained model is highly noisy it can sometimes suffice to have even just one auxiliary sample.

Thus, the current theory and practice suggests that the attacker might need at least as much data as the real training algorithm, but never needs substantially more data than that. Until this work, we knew neither whether
n
reference samples are generally necessary, nor whether they always suffice.
Our main result answers this question for a simple, natural setting by giving a lower bound on the sample complexity of membership-inference, showing that
\omega(n)
(in fact, almost
n^{2}
) samples can be necessary for an optimal attack. Thus, not only are the linear-sized reference data sets used by existing attacks necessary in natural settings, they may be insufficient.

Main Conceptual Result:

There are simple, natural settings where any successful membership-inference attack requires
\omega(n)
samples from the population—that is, many more samples than were used to train the model.

We formulate and prove our result in the simple-yet-fundamental case of mean estimation, which has long been an important testbed for theoretical work on membership inference
[
SOJH09
,
BUV14
,
DSSUV15
]
and the basis of our theoretical understanding for richer settings. Although the result is theoretical, it has noteworthy consequences for practice. The first is that the degree of privacy an algorithm ensures in practice, at least with respect to membership-inference attacks, depends heavily on the knowledge of the attacker, which is inherently difficult to reason about or measure. The second is that the existing methodology for MIA is not necessarily taking full advantage of knowledge the attacker might have, and thus we should be careful about interpreting empirical privacy measurements that are based on these potentially suboptimal attacks. We elaborate more on this perspective in
Section

1.2.1
.

1.1
Problem Setup

In this section, we introduce the setup of membership-inference attacks on mean-estimation algorithms that we use to prove our main result. To reduce the notational burden, we will define our model of MIA in a very concrete setting of Gaussian mean estimation so that we can state our main result, but later on we will define a more general setup for studying the sample-complexity of MIA.

Gaussian mean estimation.

We consider the fundamental setting of
Gaussian mean estimation
. Let the population be
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
where
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\succ 0}
is a positive-definite covariance matrix. Let
S_{n}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes n}
be a training dataset of size
n
. We consider an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that releases
\hat{\mu}=\mathcal{A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{n}}}\right)
, and, for some parameter
\rho>0
, we say the algorithm is
\rho
-accurate
if

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}-\mu}}\right\|^{2}_{\Sigma}}}\right]\leq d\rho^{2}+\frac{d}{n}.

In this definition, we measure the error in
Mahalanobis norm

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}-\mu}}\right\|_{\Sigma}=\|\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\mu}}\right)\|_{2}
. We consider this the natural way to measure error for Gaussian mean estimation because (1) it tightly captures the error from sampling, and (2) there are many differentially private algorithms
[
BGSUZ21
,
BHS23
,
KDH23
]
that ensure privacy by adding noise whose covariance is roughly proportional to
\Sigma
. Note that we define the error to account for both the standard sampling error, which is
d/n
, and any additional error whose magnitude is controlled by
\rho
. For instance, it can be achieved by setting
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}+\rho Z
where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
.

Sampled-based MIA.

Next, we formalize the problem of membership-inference attack that will study, which we refer to as
sample-based MIA.
As in all MIA, the attacker is given the model
\hat{\mu}
returned by the algorithm, and a target sample. The target sample will either be:

1.

a random member
X_{i}
in the dataset for
i
chosen uniformly in
[n]
(the “IN” case), or

2.

a non-member of the dataset
X_{0}
that is drawn independently from
\mathcal{D}
, (the “OUT” case)

or will be (the “IN” case) , and the goal is to distinguish these two cases. To do so , we will also give the attacker some partial knowledge of the distribution, which we represent through a set of
m
additional, independent samples from
\mathcal{D}
.

In this setup, a MIA is a function
\psi_{m}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{\text{IN},\text{OUT}\}
. We say
\psi_{m}
is an
m
-sample-based MIA for Gaussian mean estimators
if,
for every normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
and every
\rho
-accurate
symmetric
1
1

1

An algorithm
\mathcal{A}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is symmetric if for every dataset
S_{n}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}
, its output can be written as
\mathcal{A}(S_{n})=\bar{\mathcal{A}}(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})
for an algorithm
\bar{\mathcal{A}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, we have the following: let
(X_{0},X_{1},\dots,X_{n},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m})\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes n+m+1}
and
\hat{\mu}=\mathcal{A}(S_{n})
where
S_{n}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
. Then, it satisfies

1.

(Low FPR)
\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\psi_{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{0}}}\right)=\text{IN}}}\right)\leq 0.49
, and

2.

(High TPR) for every
i\in[n],\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\psi_{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{i}}}\right)=\text{IN}}}\right)\geq 0.51
.

To be concrete, and to make our results cleanest to state, our definition makes a few specific choices of how to quantify successful membership-inference attacks and how to specialize it to the problem we study. We note that our method and our main conceptual contribution do not seem sensitive to these choices.

1.

This definition only requires the attacker to perform slightly better than a random guess. Since we are proving lower bounds, this only makes our results stronger. Empirical work on MIA often focuses on the range of small FPR (say 1%). Our lower bounds apply in such settings—they rule out
any attack in which the difference between TPR and FPR is above a small constant.

2.

Our definition requires the attacker to succeed against an arbitrary symmetric algorithm with a given level of accuracy, but our lower bound applies to what we believe is the most natural algorithm, which returns
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}+\rho Z
where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
.

Given this model, we can succinctly state the main technical question of this work:

What is the minimum number of samples
m
, as a function of
n,d,\rho
,

that suffice for sample-based MIA for Gaussian mean estimation?

1.2
Our Results

Our main result is a strong lower bound on the sample complexity of MIA for Gaussian mean estimation, which answers the main technical question in most natural parameter regimes. Specifically, we show a large gap between the power of fully informed attackers who know the population and those who only have a limited number of samples, and specifically showing that
\omega(n)
samples can be necessary to mount an effective attack.

To put our results in context, we will establish an important baseline of a fully informed attacker (in our formalism, an attacker with
m=\infty
auxiliary samples). In this setting
[
DSSUV15
]
showed
2
2

2

Technically,
[
DSSUV15
]
considered mean estimation with discrete data in
\{0,1\}^{d}
, but since their methods apply straightforwardly to the case of Gaussian mean estimation with a fully informed attacker, we believe it is appropriate to attribute this statement to their work.
that there is a successful MIA provided that the dimension of the data is
d\gtrsim d^{\star}(n,\rho)\triangleq n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
. Moreover, when
d\lesssim d^{\star}(n,\rho)
, there is no successful MIA, even if the attacker has full knowledge of the population. Thus the dimension, and its relationship to the size of the training data, and the accuracy, is the key parameter that determines whether MIA is possible for a fully informed attacker. Therefore, an important regime to understand is when we
d\approx d^{\star}(n,\rho)
, which is the setting where MIA is possible for an optimal, fully informed attacker, and we want to understand how many samples we need to approximately match the performance of this attacker.

Our main result shows that any attacker that succeeds in this optimal regime where
d\approx d^{\star}(n,\rho)
, requires a large number of samples
m\gtrsim n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
. Note that the interesting case is when
\rho\gg n^{-1/2}
so that
m\gg n
. Moreover, the attacker still needs approximately this many samples, even if the dimension is arbitrary large. That is, we cannot trade samples for a higher dimension. This result stands in stark contrast with the case that the attacker
knows
the covariance of the population, but not the mean as we discuss in
Section

1.2.2
.

Theorem 1.1

(Main Result, Informal Version of
Theorem

3.1
)
.

Fix
n,\rho,d
, and assume
d\gtrsim d^{\star}(n,\rho)\triangleq n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
, i.e., the dimension is large enough for a fully-informed attacker to succeed.
Then, every sample-based MIA for the Gaussian mean estimation problem (
Section

1.1
) requires
m\gtrsim n^{2}\rho^{2}+n

samples. Moreover, the lower bound holds even when we restrict attention to the specific mean estimator
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}+\rho Z
where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
.

In particular, in the regime where
\rho=\omega(n^{-1/2})
—that is, when the error of the mean estimator is larger than the sampling error—
Theorem

1.1
implies that a successful attack requires
m\gtrsim n^{2}\rho^{2}=\omega(n)
. Roughly speaking, any error level
\rho
less than 1 (which could be achieved by just looking at a single sample) is meaningful. The required size of the reference sample can therefore be as large as
\Omega(n^{2})
.

We give an overview of the intuition for and techniques used to prove the main result in
Section

1.3
.

Tightness of
Theorem

1.1
.

Our main result gives completely characterizes the sample complexity of MIA for Gaussian mean estimation
in the regime where
d\approx d^{\star}(n,\rho)
, that is, when the dimension is comparable to the threshold at which MIA becomes possible for an adversary with perfect knowledge of the distribution.In general, however, the best known attack, which estimates the covariance matrix, uses
\Theta(d)
reference samples. When
d
is much larger than
d^{\star}(n,\rho)
, there is a gap between that bound and the lower bound of
Theorem

1.1
.

1.2.1
Implications for Practical MIA and Auditing

We can now elaborate on the implications of our results for practical membership-inference attacks and privacy auditing. In general, a membership-inference attack in our setting, with full knowledge of the distribution, can be written as follows: given a target point
X
and an estimated mean
\hat{\mu}
, the test
accepts
(predicts
IN
) if

\psi(X,\hat{\mu},\mathcal{D})\geq\tau(X,\mathcal{D}),

(1)

where
\mathcal{D}
is the population,
\psi
is some real-valued
test statistic
that assigns a measure of the likelihood that
X
was in the training data for
\hat{\mu}
, and
\tau
is a real-valued
threshold
that we use to make a binary prediction of IN or OUT. Given some
\phi
and
\tau
, we can try to approximate their behavior using auxiliary samples
\bm{Y}^{m}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes m}
rather than the full distribution, and our results show that doing so may require a large number of samples.

The prevailing approach to designing sample-based membership-inference attacks is to use a
restricted
form of test that
accepts
(predicts
IN
) if

\psi(X,\hat{\mu})\geq\tau(X,\mathcal{D})\,.

(2)

The difference with (
1
) is that
\mathcal{D}
no longer appears on the left-hand side.
That is, these tests compute a
distribution-independent test statistic
and use knowledge of the distribution only to calibrate the appropriate threshold.
In some settings, such attacks are known to be exactly optimal
[
SDSOJ19
]
. It is not difficult to show that if the threshold is calibrated to achieve a specific, constant FPR like 5%, then we can find such a threshold by training
O(1)
independent models (i.e. recomputing independent estimates of
\mu
a constant number of times), which requires
O(n)
samples; in practice, this is essentially how the auxiliary samples are used in calibrating the attack.

In other words, for any test of the restricted form (
2
), having
\Theta(n)
samples from the distribution is as good as having the distribution itself. However, since we can show that having
\omega(n)
samples allows for successful attacks in cases where
O(n)
samples are insufficient for a successful attack, we know that tests of the general form (
1
) can be dramatically more powerful. Therefore, it is possible that existing privacy audits based on membership-inference attacks are underestimating privacy risk, and we should be exploring attacks that exploit knowledge of the distribution in new ways to obtain better attacks. Attacks based on a quadratic approximation
[
CG24
,
SZE24
]
to the loss function, localized near the output parameters, offer a promising approach, even though they do not necessarily perform well on the specific mean estimation problem we consider.
3
3

3

Specifically, the loss being minimized in mean estimation, namely the average squared Euclidean distance from the parameter to the data points, has a data-independent Hessian. As a result, the test derived from the approaches in
[
CLNSS23
,
SZE24
]
would not perform well.

1.2.2
MIA for Mean Estimation with Known Covariance.

We complement our results by giving a characterization of membership-inference attacks when the attacker
knows
the covariance of the population, but not the mean. That is, the population is now assumed to be
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
where
\mu
is
unknown to the attacker
and
\Sigma
is
known to the attacker
. In this setting, the threshold where MIA becomes possible for a fully informed attacker remains
d^{\star}(n,\rho)=n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
.
[
DSSUV15
]
studied sample-based MIA for this setting, showing that the sample complexity can be very small, and in some cases even a single sample suffices.
4
4

4

Technically,
[
DSSUV15
]
considered mean estimation with discrete data in
\{0,1\}^{d}
, but since their methods apply straightforwardly to the case of Gaussian mean estimation with known covariance, we believe it is appropriate to attribute the statement to their work.
Specifically, they showed that if
d\gtrsim d^{\star}(n,\rho)
, then it suffices for the attacker to have
O(\newmin\{n,1/\rho^{2}\})
auxiliary samples. We show that their attack has optimal sample complexity.

Theorem 1.2

(Informal Version of
Theorem

4.1
)
.

Fix
n,\rho,d
and assume that
d\approx d^{\star}(n,\rho)=n+n^{2}\rho^{2}.
Then every sample-based MIA for Gaussian mean estimation
with known covariance
requires
m\gtrsim\newmin\{n,1/\rho^{2}\}
samples. More generally, for
n,m,d,\rho
, there exists an
m
-sample-based MIA only if
d\gtrsim n+n^{2}\rho^{2}+n^{2}/m
. Moreover, the lower bound holds even when
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}+\rho Z
where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
.

Contrasting this result with
Theorem

1.1
, we see that the driver of high sample complexity in MIA is estimating the covariance of the population.

1.3
Technical Overview

In this section, we give an overview of the technical ideas behind the proofs in the paper. Recall that the class of populations we consider in this paper is
\mathcal{P}_{d}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu,\Sigma}}\right):\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\text{$\Sigma$ is {Positive Definite}(PD)}}}\right\}
. As mentioned in
Section

1.2
, the mean estimator that witnesses the lower bound in
Theorems

1.1
and
1.2
simply computes the empirical mean and adds independent noise
\rho Z
for
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
, i.e.,
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}+\rho Z
.

First, consider an informed-attacker with the knowledge of population. Then, the informed attacker can compute the optimal test based on Neyman-Pearson Lemma which is given by

\psi_{\text{NP}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X,\hat{\mu}}}\right)=\begin{cases}\text{IN}&\text{if}~~~(X-\mu)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\hat{\mu}-\mu)\geq c\frac{d}{n},\\
\text{OUT}&\text{if}~~~(X-\mu)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\hat{\mu}-\mu)<c\frac{d}{n},\end{cases}

(3)

where
c
is a universal constant. One can show that this test will successfully distinguish the IN and OUT case (with small constant TPR and FPR) as long as
d\gtrsim d^{\star}(n,\rho)=n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
.

Attempt 1: Learning covariance matrix.

Assume without loss of generality that
\mu=0
. An observation about
Equation

3
is that the threshold is a population-independent quantity. The first attempt to design a sample-based MIA is to approximate
\Sigma^{-1}
using auxiliary samples. As we show in the proof of
Theorem

3.3
, given a matrix
H\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma^{1/2}H\Sigma^{1/2}-\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right\|_{\text{op}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we can design a test with the score function
(X-\mu)^{\top}H(\hat{\mu}-\mu)
. However, learning such
H
using auxiliary samples requires
\Omega(d)
samples
[
Ver18
, Thm. 4.7.1]
.

Attempt 2: Learning NP test statistics.

A tempting idea is based on the observation that the test statistics, i.e.,
(X-\mu)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\hat{\mu}-\mu)
is a single scalar. A natural idea is to
directly
approximate the score function without learning the covariance matrix which is a
d\times d
object (Notice that the attacker has access to both
X
and
\hat{\mu}
and we assumed
\mu=0
). Rather surprisingly, in
Appendix

A
, we show that this strategy also requires
\Omega(d)
samples. More precisely, consider the following estimation task: fix
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, every algorithm that provides an estimate
\hat{m}
of
y_{1}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}y_{2}
such that
\frac{1}{2}y_{1}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}y_{2}\leq\hat{m}\leq\frac{3}{2}y_{1}^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}y_{2}
(with a high probability) with only sampling access to
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
requires at least
\Omega(d)
samples. This sample complexity is the same as learning
\Sigma
in the operator norm!

The failure of these two natural attempts do not imply our lower bound in
Theorem

1.1
. How to generalize impossibility result to arbitrary sample-based MIAs?

1.3.1
Our lower bound for arbitrary sample-based MIAs.

Consider
Equation

3
. Intuitively,
(X-\mu)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(\hat{\mu}-\mu)
measures the correlation between the data point and the estimated mean, within the geometry induced by
\Sigma
. The crucial component is in
Equation

3
is
\Sigma^{-1}
. It re-weights the space to account for the data variance, ensuring the test measures meaningful correlation rather than misleading large correlation resulting from high variance directions. Intuitively, it suggests that a sample-based MIA may need to find any directions with high variance in the data to differentiate noise from the signal. This is exactly the intuition behind our lower bound in
Theorem

1.1
. We consider a specific class of covariance matrices: we consider the family of Gaussian populations with spiked covariance matrix:

\mathcal{P}_{(d,k)-\text{spiked}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma):\Sigma=\mathbb{I}_{d}+\sigma^{2}UU^{\top}~\text{where}~U\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}~\text{with orthonormal columns}}}\right\}

(4)

where we assume that
\sigma^{2}\gtrsim d
. The intuition behind this structure is that when there are
k
high-variance directions, an attacker with
m<k
auxiliary samples cannot identify all the high-variance directions. Then, we show that without identifying all the high-variance directions an attacker cannot succeed.

To prove this result formally, we use
Section

2.3
. The Gaussian distribution is characterized by its mean
\mu
and covariance
\Sigma
. Therefore, we can define a family of hypothesis testing problems parameterized by
\Sigma
as follows:

\displaystyle H_{(\mu,\Sigma)}=\begin{cases}H_{\text{OUT},(\mu,\Sigma)}&:(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{0})\sim P_{\text{OUT},(\mu,\Sigma)},\\
H_{\text{IN},(\mu,\Sigma)}&:(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{1})\sim P_{\text{IN},(\mu,\Sigma)}.\end{cases}

Here,
P_{\text{OUT},(\mu,\Sigma)}
and
P_{\text{IN},(\mu,\Sigma)}
are the joint distribution of
(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{0})
and
(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{1})
, respectively (see
Definition

2.1
for more formal description.). Our goal is to prove a lower bound to capture an attacker that doesn’t know
(\mu,\Sigma)
. To do so, we use the following result: for every distribution over
(\mu,\Sigma)
denoted by
\pi
and attacker’s strategy
\psi_{m}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{\text{IN},\text{OUT}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\newsup_{(\mu,\Sigma)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{P_{\text{OUT},(\mu,\Sigma)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\psi_{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{0}}}\right)=\text{IN}}}\right)+P_{\text{IN},(\mu,\Sigma)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\psi_{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{1}}}\right)=\text{OUT}}}\right)}}\right\}\geq 1-\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}P_{\text{OUT},\pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}P_{\text{IN},\pi}}}}\right)},

where
P_{\text{IN},\pi}
(and similarly
P_{\text{OUT},\pi}
) is a mixture distribution where the sampling procedure is based on first sampling
(\mu,\Sigma)\sim\pi
, and then sampling
(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{1})
from
P_{\text{IN},(\mu,\Sigma)}
. This implies that if we can find a distribution
\pi
and show that

\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}P_{\text{OUT},\pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}P_{\text{IN},\pi}}}}\right)}\leq 0.02,

we can show that for every
m
-sample-based MIA, there exists a population such that the attacker can’t satisfy the minimal accuracy conditions.

For the
unknown covariance setting
we consider the distribution
\pi
to be uniform distribution over all the subspaces of dimension
k
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see
Equation

4
). Then, we need to characterize the total variation (TV) distance between two mixture populations. Characterizing TV (or KL) between mixture populations is challenging and there is no general approach for it. To do so, our proof relies on two key technical steps: The first step is a reduction that shows given
k>m
, we can relate the performance of
m
-sample-based MIA to a sample-based attacker with
zero auxiliary sample in
\mathbb{R}^{d-2m}
with
k-m
high-variance directions
. This step significantly simplifies the proof. More formally, let
(\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},X_{1},\hat{\mu})=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d,k,m,\sigma}}\right)
and
(\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}})=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d-2m,k-m,0,\sigma}}\right)
where
Sample
is the sampling procedure from the mixture distribution (see
Algorithm

1
for a formal description.). More precisely, we have

\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}}}}\right)}\leq c\sqrt{\frac{m}{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}+\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}}}}}\right)}.

(5)

This step implies that as long as
m\lesssim n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
, the first term is small. The second technical step concerns with the zero auxiliary sample case, i.e., the second term in
Equation

5
. In particular, we show a structural result which implies that
the sufficient statistic
for attackers with zero auxiliary samples has a very simple form. We show that the sufficient statistics are given by norm of the data point, norm of the output, and the inner product between the data point and the output vector. More precisely,

\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}}}}}\right)}=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{X_{0}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{\hat{\mu}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{0}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}}}}}}}}}\right>}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{X_{1}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{\hat{\mu}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{1}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}}}}}}}}}\right>}}}\right)}.

(6)

To bound the RHS, we use the chain rule for TV
Lemma

D.1
. This step consists of various technical steps. For example, we show a bound on the the expectation of ratio of Gaussian quadratic forms, i.e.,

\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{(Z^{T}AZ)^{2}}{Z^{T}A^{2}Z}}}\right]

for a symmetric matrix
A
as well as the total variation distance between perturbed chi-squared random variables, i.e., the TV distance between
X_{1}+Y
and
X_{2}+Y
where
Y
follows chi-squared distribution and
X_{1},X_{2}
are random variables that are independent of
Y
.

For the
known covariance setting
, we define the mixture probability
\pi
over
\mu
as
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})
. The key insight is that conditioning on auxiliary samples yields tractable posterior distribution, allowing us to compute the total variation distance explicitly. The key step is that under the two hypotheses, the distribution of auxiliary samples are the same. Thus, by the chain rule for KL divergence,

\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}}}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}X_{1},\hat{\mu}\big|\bm{Y}^{m}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}X_{0},\hat{\mu}\big|\bm{Y}^{m}}}}\right)}}\right].

Conditioning on auxiliary samples
\bm{Y}^{m}=\mathbf{y}^{m}
only affects the distribution of
\mu
. In particular, we show that
X_{1},\hat{\mu}|\bm{Y}^{m}
and
X_{0},\hat{\mu}|\bm{Y}^{m}
both follow Gaussian distribution and this observation lets us to obtain a closed form expression for the KL distance.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notation

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}
. For a given matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
,
\mathrm{Im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)=\{y:\exists x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}~\text{s.t.}~Ax=y\}
. Also for a set of vectors
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}
, we write
\text{span}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}}}\right)=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\exists(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m},y=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}x_{i}\}
. For a given matrix
A
, we denote by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|A}}\right\|_{\text{op}}=\newmax_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},x\neq 0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Ax}}\right\|}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|}}}\right\}
where
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\cdot}}\right\|
denotes the standard
\ell_{2}
norm, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|A}}\right\|_{F}^{2}=\mathrm{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A^{\top}A}}\right)
where
\mathrm{tr}(A)
denotes the trace of matrix
A
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a measurable space (equipped with an implicit
\sigma
-algebra), we write
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{Z})
to denote the set of probability measures over
\mathcal{Z}
. For two random variables
X
and
Y
defined on the same probability space, we write
X~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~Y
to denote
X
and
Y
are equal in distribution.

For probability measures
P
and
Q
defined on the same measurable space, we denote their Total Variation (TV) distance and Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence as follows:

•

The TV distance is defined as
\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Q}}}\right)}=\newsup_{A}|P(A)-Q(A)|
, where the supremum is taken over all measurable sets
A
.

•

The KL divergence is defined as
\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}Q}}}\right)=\int\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{dP}{dQ}(x)}}\right)dP(x)
, which is finite only if
P
is absolutely continuous with respect to
Q
.

We also use the following notations: for
X\sim P
and
Y\sim Q
, we use
\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y}}}\right)}=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Q}}}\right)}
and
\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}X}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}Y}}}\right)=\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}Q}}}\right)
. For a pair of jointly distributed random variables
(X_{1},Y_{1})
and
(X_{2},Y_{2})
, we use the following (non-standard) notation for the conditional TV and KL distance

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}Y_{1}\Big|{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y_{2}\Big|{X_{2}}}}}\right)}}}\right]=\int_{x}\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}Y_{1}\Big|{X_{1}=x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y_{2}\Big|{X_{2}=x}}}}\right)}\text{d}P_{X_{1}}(x),

\hfill\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}Y_{1}\Big|X_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}Y_{2}\Big|X_{2}}}}\right)}}\right]=\int_{x}\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}Y_{1}\Big|X_{1}=x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}Y_{2}\Big|X_{2}=x}}}\right)\text{d}P_{X_{1}}(x),

where
P_{X_{1}}
denotes the marginal distribution of
X_{1}
.
Notice that the expectation is over the distribution of
X_{1}
. We denote by
\chi^{2}(k)
the chi-square distribution with
k
degrees of freedom, i.e., the distribution of the sum of squares of
k
independent mean-zero and unit-variance Gaussian random variables.

2.2
Minimal Definition of Membership Inference

In this section, we present a definition for the membership inference attack. Our definition captures a realistic attack scenario where the attacker has limited knowledge: rather than having access to the true data distribution, the attacker possesses only
m
auxiliary samples drawn from the same distribution i.i.d.. The goal is to achieve membership inference performance that is slightly better than random guessing. Now, we are ready to formally define minimal
sample-based MIA.

Definition 2.1

(
(\mathcal{P},\mathfrak{A},n,m)
-sample-based MIA)
.

Fix
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a family of probability distributions over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
\mathfrak{A}
be a class of mean estimation algorithms, where each algorithm
\mathcal{A}\in\mathfrak{A}
maps a dataset of size
n
to an estimate in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. A function
\psi_{m}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{\mathrm{IN},\mathrm{OUT}\}
is a
(\mathcal{P},\mathfrak{A},n,m)
-sample-based MIA if the following holds: For every algorithm
\mathcal{A}\in\mathfrak{A}
and every distribution
\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{P}

•

Sample
(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes(m+n+1)}
.

•

Let
\bm{Y}^{m}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})
denote the auxiliary samples.

•

Let
\hat{\mu}=\mathcal{A}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
be the algorithm’s output.

Then the attack must satisfy

\mathrm{Pr}(\psi_{m}(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{0})=\mathrm{OUT})\geq 0.51,\quad\mathrm{Pr}(\psi_{m}(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X_{1})=\mathrm{IN})\geq 0.51.

Remark 2.2
.

We make a note in passing that even though we present the definition for the mean estimation algorithms, the above definition can be generalized to every learning tasks.

In this paper, our main focus is on the following sets of probability distributions:

Definition 2.3
.

Fix
d\in\mathbb{N}
. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{d}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu,\Sigma}}\right):\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\text{$\Sigma$ is {Positive Definite}}}}\right\}.

We also define
\mathcal{P}^{\text{id.cov}}_{d}\subset\mathcal{P}_{d}
as
\mathcal{P}^{\text{id.cov}}_{d}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu,\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right):\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\right\}
. In particular, all the distributions in
\mathcal{P}^{\text{id.cov}}_{d}
have the identity covariance matrix.

Noisy empirical mean estimator and MIA on it.

In the next definition, we introduce a particular mean estimator that plays an important role in the sequel.

Definition 2.4
.

Fix
d,n\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\rho>0
. Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
be a Gaussian distribution with mean
\mu
and covariance
\Sigma
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{n,\rho}
be an estimator that given
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes(n)}
outputs
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}+\rho Z
where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
such that
Z\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
. This algorithm satisfies
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}-\mu}}\right\|^{2}_{\Sigma}}}\right]\leq d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{2}+\frac{1}{n}}}\right)
where
\rho>0
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}-\mu}}\right\|_{\Sigma}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\mu}}\right)}}\right\|_{2}
.

Let us consider MIA on
\mathcal{A}_{n,\rho}
. To establish a performance benchmark, consider an attacker with access to infinite number of auxiliary samples – equivalently, complete knowledge of the data distribution. The following proposition characterizes the performance of such an idealized attacker. We skip the proof as the first part is from
[
DSSUV15
]
, and the second part follows by simple calculations.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
c
be a universal constant. Fix
d,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
Let
\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
. Let
(X_{0},X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes(n+1)}
and
\hat{\mu}=\mathcal{A}_{n,\rho}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
. Then,

1.

Given
d\geq c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\right)
, there exists an attacker with the full knowledge of the data distribution, i.e.,
m\to\infty
in
Definition

2.1
, that can succeed in the sense of
Definition

2.1
.

2.

We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{1},\hat{\mu}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{0},\hat{\mu}}}}\right)}\leq\sqrt{\frac{d}{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}.

2.3
Preliminaries on Establishing Lower Bound for Hypothesis Testing

This paper aims to establish information-theoretic bounds on the number of auxiliary samples required for minimal sample-based MIA (
Definition

2.1
). Our main tool is the following lemma.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
V
and
\mathcal{Z}
be measurable spaces. Consider two arbitrary sets of probability measures
\{P_{0,\nu}\}_{\nu\in V}
and
\{P_{1,\nu}\}_{\nu\in V}
over
\mathcal{Z}
. Consider an arbitrary distribution
\pi
over
V
. Also, define the mixture probability measures of
P_{0,\pi}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\nu\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{0,\nu}}}\right]
and
P_{1,\pi}\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{\nu\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{1,\nu}}}\right]
. Then, consider the family of hypothesis testing problems
\{H_{\nu}=(H_{0,\nu},H_{1,\nu})\}_{\nu\in V}
where for every
\nu\in V
,

\displaystyle H_{\nu}=\begin{cases}H_{0,\nu}&:Z\sim P_{0,\nu},\\
H_{1,\nu}&:Z\sim P_{1,\nu}.\end{cases}

Then, for every
\pi
and decision rule (test)
\Psi:\mathcal{Z}\to\{0,1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\newsup_{\nu\in V}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{P_{0,\nu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Psi^{-1}(1)}}\right)+P_{1,\nu}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Psi^{-1}(0)}}\right)}}\right\}\geq 1-\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}P_{0,\pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}P_{1,\pi}}}}\right)}.

This lemma provides a systematic approach for proving lower bounds. The key steps are:

1.

Specify a family of hypothesis testing problems
\{H_{\nu}=(H_{0,\nu},H_{1,\nu})\}_{\nu\in V}
where
\nu
is a parameter unknown to the tester. In our application of Gaussian mean estimation, the unknown parameters of the distributions are
(\mu,\Sigma)
.

2.

Specify a prior distribution
\pi
over the parameters
\{(\mu,\Sigma):\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\}
.

3.

Provide an upper bound on
\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}P_{0,\pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}P_{1,\pi}}}}\right)}
where
P_{0,\pi}
and
P_{1,\pi}
are mixture distributions (See
Lemma

2.6
.).

The bound then follows from
Lemma

2.6
, showing that any test’s sum of probability errors under the two hypotheses is at least
1-\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}P_{0,\pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}P_{1,\pi}}}}\right)}
for some value of
\nu
. In particular, as per
Definition

2.1
, if
\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}P_{0,\pi}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}P_{1,\pi}}}}\right)}\leq 0.02
, then, no sample-based MIA can satisfy the accuracy requirement in
Definition

2.1
.

3
Sample-based MIA with Unknown Covariance

In this section, we present our results on characterization of the minimum number of samples required for any sample-based MIA to satisfy the minimal accuracy conditions in
Definition

2.1
, in the case that both the mean and covariance of the data distribution are unknown to the attacker. Later in
Section

4
, we show how the minimum number of auxiliary samples changes if the covariance of the data distribution is known to the attacker.

Lower bound: minimum number of samples required by any attacker.

First, we present a lower bound on the minimum number of samples required by any attacker in the unknown covariance case to satisfy
Definition

2.1
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
be universal constants. Recall the definition of sample-based MIA in
Definition

2.1
. Let
d,n,m\in\mathbb{N},\rho\in\mathbb{R}
be arbitrary constants that satisfy,
d>c_{1}(n+n^{2}\rho^{2})
, and
m\leq c_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\right)
. Then, there is
no

(\mathcal{P}_{d},\{\mathcal{A}_{n,\rho}\},n,m)
-sample-based MIA where
\mathcal{A}_{n,\rho}
is the noisy empirical mean defined in
Definition

2.4
and
\mathcal{P}_{d}
is the distribution family of defined in
Definition

2.3
.

The implication of this result is as follows: when the mean estimator is simply the empirical mean plus Gaussian noise from
Definition

2.4
, every sample-based MIA requires a number of auxiliary samples which is
\Omega\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\right)
. This holds given certain conditions on the dimension. Note that when
d\geq\Omega(n+n^{2}\rho^{2})
, an informed attacker who knows the data distribution satisfies the accuracy condition (see
Proposition

2.5
). Also, notice that when the additive error is larger than the sampling error, i.e.,
\rho\geq 1/\sqrt{n}
, the number of auxiliary samples required is
\Omega\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\right)
, i.e., it is quadratic in the number of training samples.

Upperbound: Sample-based MIA Strategy.

In this section, we provide a sample-based MIA strategy for the unknown covariance case. First, we define a general class of mean estimators:

Definition 3.2
.

Fix
d,n,\rho
such that
\rho<1
. Define
\mathfrak{A}_{n,d,\rho}
as the set of mean estimator algorithms as follows.
\mathcal{A}\in\mathfrak{A}_{n,d,\rho}
iff the following holds: Let
\mathcal{A}:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a symmetric mean estimator algorithm, i.e., for every dataset
S_{n}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}
, its output can be written as
\mathcal{A}(S_{n})=\bar{\mathcal{A}}(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})
for a (possibly randomized) function
\bar{\mathcal{A}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, for every
\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
with covariance matrix
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{S_{n}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes n},\hat{\mu}\sim\mathcal{A}(S_{n})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}}}\right)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}}}\right)\leq 2\rho^{2}d}}\right)\geq 0.99.

Note the noisy empirical mean in
Definition

2.4
is a member of
\mathfrak{A}_{n,d,\rho}
. Also, by a simple triangle inequality and union bound, we have
\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\mu}}\right)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\mu}}\right)\leq c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{2}+\frac{1}{n}}}\right)d}}\right)\geq 0.95
for a universal constant
c
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
c>1
be a universal constant. Fix
d,n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\rho<1
. Then, given
m\geq cd
and
d\geq c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\right)
, there exists an
(\mathcal{P}_{d},\mathfrak{A}_{n,d,\rho},n,m)
-sample-based MIA in the sense of
Definition

2.1
where
\mathcal{P}_{d}
defined in
Definition

2.3
and
\mathfrak{A}_{n,d,\rho}
defined in
Definition

3.2
.

The strategy of the attacker is quite simple and inspired by
[
DSSUV15
]
: The attacker divides its auxiliary samples into three parts of size
\Theta(d)
,
\Theta(\newmin\{n,1/\rho^{2}\})
, and one. Using
\Theta(d)
samples it constructs the empirical covariance matrix. Let us denote the inverse of the empirical covariance matrix by
H
. Then, it uses the next
\Theta(\newmin\{n,1/\rho^{2}\})
samples to get an estimate of the unknown mean. Let us denote it by
\bar{\mu}_{0}
. Also, denote the last held-out point by
Y_{0}
. Then, the strategy of the attacker is

\displaystyle\psi_{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X}}\right)=\begin{cases}\text{IN}&\text{if }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{H^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)},{H^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right)}}}}}}}\right>\geq c\frac{d}{n}\\
\text{OUT}&\text{if }\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{H^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)},{H^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right)}}}}}}}\right><c\frac{d}{n}\end{cases}

where
c
is a universal constant.

Remark 3.4
.

To compare the lower bound in
Theorem

3.1
and the upper bound in
Theorem

3.3
, consider the case that
d\asymp d^{\star}(n,\rho)=n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
. Note that
d\asymp d^{\star}(n,\rho)
is the threshold under which even an attacker with the full knowledge of the distribution cannot succeed
[
DSSUV15
]
. In this regime, the lower bound in
Theorem

3.1
shows that any sample-based MIA requires at least
m\geq d^{\star}(n,\rho)
. Also, the upper bound in
Theorem

3.3
shows there exists an attacker which requires
O(d^{\star}(n,\rho))
samples. Therefore, in the regime that
d\asymp d^{\star}(n,\rho)
, our result is tight.

3.1
Proof Sketch of
Theorem

3.1

Proof Sketch of
Theorem

3.1
.

In this part, we provide an informal overview of the proof, highlighting the key steps.

Setup.

Since we want to prove a lower bound, we assume that the mean of the unknown data distribution is zero. We consider the following family of data distributions where the covariance structure is as follows:
Let
\sigma>0
be a constant and
k,d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k\leq d
. Then, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{(d,k)-\text{spiked}}=\Big\{\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma):

\displaystyle\Sigma=\mathbb{I}_{d}+\sigma^{2}UU^{\top}~\text{where}~U\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}~\text{with orthonormal columns}

\displaystyle\text{\text{and} $UU^{\top}$ is the projection matrix onto a rank-$k$ subspace}\Big\}.

Intuitively, each distribution in
\mathcal{P}_{(d,k)-\text{spiked}}
has a large variance on a subspace of dimension
k
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Based on the recipe in
Section

2.3
, we need to specify a prior distribution over this class of covariance matrices. We consider the prior distribution, denoted by
\pi_{k,d}
, which is the uniform distribution over the subspaces of dimension
k
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The sampling process is formally presented in
Algorithm

1
.

Algorithm 1

Data Generation with Unknown Covariance:
\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d,k,m,\sigma}}\right)

1:

number of samples:
n
, total dimension:
d
, number of high variance dimensions:
k
, the number of auxiliary samples:
m
, the variance parameter:
\sigma
.

2:

Let
\pi_{k,d}
be uniform distribution over the subspaces of dimension
k
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

3:

Sample
UU^{\top}
from
\pi_{k,d}
.

4:

Let
\Sigma=\mathbb{I}_{d}+\sigma^{2}UU^{\top}
.

5:

Sample
(X_{0},X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Z,Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{m})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)^{\otimes(n+m+2)}
.

6:

Let
\bm{Y}^{m}=(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{m})
.

7:

Let
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}+\rho Z
.

8:

Let
\hat{\mu}_{0}=\hat{\mu}_{1}=\hat{\mu}
.

9:

Output

(\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1})
.

Reduction to zero auxiliary sample.

The first step is a reduction which shows that the performance of an attacker with access to
m
auxiliary samples can be related to the performance of another sample-based MIA with
zero auxiliary samples
for a
different problem
. Here, we give an overview of the reduction: Assume that the attacker has access to the auxiliary samples
\bm{Y}^{m}=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m})
from an unknown data distribution. Using this
m
samples, we define a sequence of
m
random variables
\bm{Y}^{m}_{\|}=(UU^{\top}Y_{1},\dots,UU^{\top}Y_{m})
. Notice that
UU^{\top}Y_{i}
is the projection of
Y_{i}
onto the unknown subspace with a large variance. By Gaussianity and
m\leq k
,
\bm{Y}^{m}_{\|}=(UU^{\top}Y_{1},\dots,UU^{\top}Y_{m})
is
m

independent
random vectors lie in the subspace spanned by
UU^{\top}
with probability one.

Assume that we consider an even more powerful attacker who has access to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}^{m},\bm{Y}^{m}_{\|}}}\right)
. Intuitively, using this information the attacker can identify
m
directions in the high-variance subspace. Also, by computing
\{Y_{i}-UU^{\top}Y_{i}:i\in[m]\}
, the attacker can identify
m
directions in
(UU^{\top})^{\perp}
, i.e.,
m
directions that are perpendicular to the high-variance subspace. To gain more intuition, see
Figure

1
which depicts the orthonormalization of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
from the attacker’s point of view. Therefore, the attacker knows a subspace of dimension
m
from
UU^{\top}
and a subspace of dimension
m
from
(UU^{\top})^{\perp}
. Interestingly, we show that for this more powerful attacker, the problem of membership inference reduces to the membership inference attack in
\mathbb{R}^{d-2m}
with
k-m
noisy directions when the attacker has access to
zero
auxiliary samples. Intuitively, see
Figure

1
. The proof of this reduction is based on showing that the attacker has zero information about the unknown subspaces, and in fact, the noisy
k-m
directions is distributed uniformly in
d-2m
available dimensions.

More precisely, we show the following: let
(\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{0},\hat{\mu}_{1})=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d,k,m,\sigma}}\right)
and
(\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}})=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d-2m,k-m,0,\sigma}}\right)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}\right)}\leq c\sqrt{\frac{m}{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}+\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}\right)}.

This inequality shows that given that
m\lesssim n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
, the first term can be smaller than an arbitrary constant.

m
known
directions
k-m
unknown
directions
m
known
directions
d-k-m
unknown
directions
noisy subspace
with variance
\sigma^{2}
unit variance subspace
\mathbb{R}^{d}=
d
d

Figure 1
:
Orthonormalization of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
from the point of view of the attacker with
m
auxiliary samples
(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m})
and
m
additional auxiliary samples
(UU^{\top}Y_{1},\dots,UU^{\top}Y_{m})
. The matrix on the RHS is
d\times d
and columns show an orthonormal basis for
\mathbb{R}^{d}

Sufficient statistic for zero auxiliary sample MIA.

The last step is to show that with zero auxiliary samples, it is impossible to perform MIA, i.e.,
\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}\right)}
is smaller than a constant. In particular, we need to provide an upper bound on the total variation distance between two mixture distributions. Comparing the total variation distance between two mixture distributions is a challenging task and there are no general techniques for it.

The crucial technical step in our proof is characterizing the
sufficient statistics
for zero auxiliary samples case. In zero auxiliary samples case, we show that the sufficient statistics are given by norm of the data point, norm of the output, and the inner product between the data point and the output vector. More precisely,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}\right)}=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{X_{0}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{0}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}}\right>}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{X_{1}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{1}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}}\right>}}}\right)}.

Statistical indistinguishability with zero auxiliary samples.

Characterization of the sufficient statistics significantly simplifies the proof. Intuitively, it is difficult to distinguish between two cases mainly because the norm and the inner product operators
mix
the components from the noisy and unit-variance directions, therefore, it makes the TV term small.

To be more precise, let
(\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}})=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d-2m,k-m,0,\sigma}}\right)
also recall the definition of the random variable
UU^{\top}
and its dependence to other random variables in
Algorithm

1
. We use the chain rule for TV distance from
Lemma

D.1
to write

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{X_{0}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{0}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}}\right>}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\widetilde{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{1}}}\right\|},\widetilde{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{1}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}}\right>}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{0}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|{\widetilde{X_{0}},UU^{\top}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{1}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|{\widetilde{X_{1}},UU^{\top}}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}\right\|\Big|{\widetilde{X_{0}},UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{0}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}}\right>}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|\Big|{\widetilde{X_{1}},UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{1}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}}\right>}}}}\right)}}}\right].

The intuition behind the upper bound is we make the attacker stronger by giving the vector
x
and the noisy subspace, i.e.,
UU^{\top}
to the attacker.

To gain an intuition why the TV distance is small, consider the inner product term. By definition
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{0}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right]=0
and
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{1}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{1}}}\right\|^{2}}}\right]}{n}=\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma)}{n}=\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d-k-m+(k-m)\sigma^{2}}}\right)}{n}
. Therefore, the mean of the inner product terms under two hypotheses is different. However, as we show, the variance is large and it can
hide
the difference between the means. We can show
\text{Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{0}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)\approx\text{Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\widetilde{X_{1}}},{\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)\approx\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}}\right)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma}}\right\|_{F}^{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}}\right)\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d-k-m+(k-m)\sigma^{4}}}\right)
(see
Lemma

D.11
for the proof). Assume that
(k-m)\sigma^{2}\geq d
. We can make this assumption since
(k,d,\sigma)
are parameters of our choice in the construction. Then, consider the ratio of the mean over the the standard deviation:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\hat{\mu}},{X_{1}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right]}{\sqrt{\text{Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\hat{\mu}},{X_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d-k-m+(k-m)\sigma^{2}}{\sqrt{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{d-k-m+(k-m)\sigma^{4}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d+(k-m)\sigma^{2}}{\sqrt{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{(k-m)\sigma^{4}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\lesssim}}\frac{(k-m)\sigma^{2}}{\sqrt{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{(k-m)\sigma^{4}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{k-m}}{\sqrt{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}},

where
(a)
follows since
(k-m)\sigma^{2}\geq d
. Assume we set the parameters such that
k-m
is a universal constant. Then, for a sufficiently large
n
, this ratio can be arbitrarily small. This shows that the variance in the inner product hides the difference in their mean.

For the term related to the norms under two hypotheses, the analysis is more challenging. In particular, we need to provide an upper bound on

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|\Big|X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>=r}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|\Big|X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}}\right)}.

For
i\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right\|^{2}=\Pi_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right)+\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\frac{x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|}},{\hat{\mu}_{i}}}}}}}}\right>+\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right)
, where
\Pi_{x}
and
\Pi_{x^{\perp}}
are the orthogonal projection operator on the subspace spanned by
x
and the orthogonal complement of it, respectively. Notice that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\frac{x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|}},{\hat{\mu}_{i}}}}}}}}\right>=r\frac{x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|}
. Therefore, by the invariance of TV to the one-to-one mappings,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)}}\right\|\Big|X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>=r}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)}}\right\|\Big|X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}}\right)}.

Let
v=\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\|UU^{\top}x\|}}}\right)
denote the component of the normalized projection orthogonal to
x
. Since
UU^{\top}
has rank
k
, we can choose an orthonormal basis
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}
for
\text{Im}(UU^{\top})
such that:

•

u_{1}=\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\|UU^{\top}x\|}
(the normalized projection of
x
onto the subspace)

•

u_{i}\perp x
for
i\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
(the remaining basis vectors are orthogonal to
x
)

Also, define
Z_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d}),(\xi_{1,0},\xi_{1,1},\dots,\xi_{k})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{k+1}
such that
(Z_{0},\xi_{1,0},\xi_{1,1},\dots,\xi_{k})
are mutually independent. Also,
\alpha_{0}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}
and
\alpha_{1}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{n^{2}}+\rho^{2}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{0}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\alpha_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}\xi_{1,0}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right),

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{x}{n}+\alpha_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}\xi_{1,1}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right).

Recall the specific basis vectors we pick for
\mathrm{Im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(UU^{\top}}}\right)
and its properties. Then, we decompose
\hat{\mu}_{0}
and
\hat{\mu}_{1}
on the subspace spanned by
x
and the subspace orthogonal to
x
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{0}&=\alpha_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}}}\right)+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}}}\right)\xi_{1,0}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right),\\
\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{1}&=\alpha_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}}}\right)+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}}}\right)\xi_{1,1}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right).\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>&=\alpha_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|\xi_{1,0}}}\right),\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>&=\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{n}+\alpha_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|\xi_{1,1}}}\right).\end{cases}

Observe that conditioning on the inner product induces different conditioned distribution on
\xi_{1,0}
and
\xi_{1,1}
. The main technical challenge here to show that
\sigma\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}
can hide the difference between
\xi_{1,0}
and
\xi_{1,1}
. To show that, we characterize the total variation distance between two random variables perturbed by chi-squared distribution: Let
(X_{1},X_{2},Y)
be jointly distributed random variables such that 1)
(X_{1},X_{2})\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}Y
, 2)
Y
is distributed as
\chi^{2}(k)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{1}+Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{2}+Y}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}-X_{2}|]}}\right)^{2}}{4k-16}}.

Using this technical result, we show that by having large enough noisy directions, i.e.,
k-m
, TV distance between norms can be arbitrarily small.
∎

3.2
Key Steps in the Formal Proof of
Theorem

3.1

In this section, we give a detailed proof of
Theorem

3.1
by presenting the important steps. All the proofs are deferred to
Appendix

B
.

3.2.1
Construction.

Let
\sigma>0
be a constant and
k,d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k\leq d
. We consider the following family of covariance matrices given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{(d,k)-\text{spiked}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathbb{I}_{d}+\sigma^{2}UU^{\top}:~\text{$U\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}$, $UU^{\top}$ is the projection matrix onto a rank-$k$ subspace}}}\right\}.

Following the recipe in
Section

2.3
, we need to specify a distribution over the family of
\mathcal{C}_{(d,k)-\text{spiked}}
. The chosen distribution, denoted by
\pi_{k,d}
, is the uniform distribution over the subspaces of dimension
k
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

3.2.2
Reduction to the Zero Auxiliary Sample Case

The first step is to establish a reduction showing that if membership inference with zero auxiliary samples is difficult, it remains difficult with
m
auxiliary samples, provided
m
is smaller than
n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
. The next lemma formalizes this results. The proof quite technical and involved; for an intuition see
Section

3.1
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
Sample
be given by
Algorithm

1
. Fix
d,n,m,\sigma
such that
d>2m
and
k>m
. Let
(\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{0},\hat{\mu}_{1})=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d,k,m,\sigma}}\right)
and
(\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}})=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d-2m,k-m,0,\sigma}}\right)
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}\right)}\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{m}{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}+\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}\right)}.

3.2.3
Zero Auxiliary Sample Case

After reducing the problem to the case that the number of auxiliary samples is zero, we discuss in this part how we prove that MIA is difficult with zero auxiliary samples. We first start with an important observation:

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d,k,0,\sigma}}\right)
be as defined in
Algorithm

1
. Then, for
j\in\{0,1\}
and every rotation matrix
R\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{j},\hat{\mu}_{j}}}\right)~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(RX_{j},R\hat{\mu}_{j}}}\right)
.

This observation lets us prove a structural result on the form of probability density function (PDF) of
(X_{j},\hat{\mu}_{j})
for
j\in\{0,1\}
. In particular, we show in
Lemma

B.1
that for every pair of random variables that satisfies the property in
Lemma

3.6
, the joint PDF can only depend on the norms and their inner product. This results leads to the following result on the sufficient statistics for the problem of MIA with zero auxiliary samples.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d,k,0,\sigma}}\right)
be as defined in
Algorithm

1
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}\right)}=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>}}}\right)}.

To provide an upper bound on the RHS of
Lemma

3.7
, we use the following result which is an immediate consequence of the data processing inequality and the chain rule fo TV distance.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d,k,0,\sigma}}\right)
from
Algorithm

1
. Also, recall the definition of
UU^{\top}
from
Algorithm

1
and its dependence on other random variables. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|{X_{0},UU^{\top}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|{X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|\Big|{X_{0},UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|\Big|{X_{1},UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>}}}}\right)}}}\right].

Lemma

3.8
breaks the problem of upper bounding the total variation distance into two parts. The first step is to prove an upper bound on the inner product term.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d,k,0,\sigma}}\right)
be as defined in
Algorithm

1
. Also, recall the definition of
UU^{\top}
from
Algorithm

1
and its dependence to other random variables. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|{X_{0},UU^{\top}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|{X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}}\right)}}}\right]\leq\sqrt{\frac{4}{n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((k+3)+\frac{(d-k+3)^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}(k-2)}}}\right)}.

Finally, for the term related to the norm of the ouput under the two hypotheses, we have the following technical lemma.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
n\geq 2
. Let
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d,k,0,\sigma}}\right)
be as defined in
Algorithm

1
. Also, recall the definition of
UU^{\top}
from
Algorithm

1
and its dependence to other random variables. For every
c>1
such that
n\geq 2
,
8k\leq c^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\right)
,
\sigma^{2}k\geq d
, and
d>k>4c^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|\Big|{X_{0},UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|\Big|{X_{1},UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{12c^{2}}{n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}+\frac{k}{8(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}+\frac{1024c^{4}}{k-9}}+\frac{3}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{1}{(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{2d+d^{2}}}{4(1+\sigma^{2})}+\frac{k}{8(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}\frac{2d+d^{2}}{(2k-18)(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}}.

3.2.4
Putting the Pieces Together

After describing the key lemmas towards proving the main theorem, now we discuss the parameter settings such that the claimed lower bound holds. Let
d^{\star}(n,\rho)\triangleq n+n^{2}\rho^{2}-1
In particular, from
Lemmas

3.5
,
3.8
,
3.9
and
3.10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{m}{d^{\star}(n,\rho)+1}}+\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}\right)}
