\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{m}{d^{\star}(n,\rho)+1}}+\sqrt{\frac{4}{d^{\star}(n,\rho)-1}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((k-m+3)+\frac{(d-k-m+3)^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}(k-m+2)}}}\right)}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{12c^{2}}{d^{\star}(n,\rho)}+\frac{k-m}{4(d^{\star}(n,\rho))^{2}}+\frac{525c^{4}}{k-m-4}}+\frac{3}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{d^{\star}(n,\rho)}\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{2(d-2m)+(d-2m)^{2}}}{4(1+\sigma^{2})}+\frac{k-m}{8}+\frac{2(d-2m)+(d-2m)^{2}}{(2(k-m)-18)(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}},

where for
Lemmas

3.9
and
3.10
, we adjust the values of
d
and
k
since
(\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}})=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d-2m,k-m,0,\sigma}}\right)
. Also, recall that the last inequality holds for every
c>1
such that
n\geq 2
,
8(k-m)\leq c^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\right)
,
\sigma^{2}(k-m)\geq d-2m
, and
d-2m>k-m>4c^{2}
.

We need to show that there are many parameter settings
5
5

5

Note that we do not make any attempts to optimize the constants. Obtaining the sharpest constants will be deferred to future work.
that leads to
\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}\right)}\leq 0.02
in order to prove that there is no MIA strategy for
Definition

2.1
. In particular, given
m,n,\rho,d
:

1.

We can choose
c=35
. Then,
3/c^{2}\leq\frac{0.01}{4}
.

2.

For the number of auxiliary samples, we need
400m\leq n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
leads to
2\sqrt{\frac{m}{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\leq 0.01
.

3.

For the number of high variance direction:
k=m+N_{\text{dir}}
where
N_{\text{dir}}
is a universal constant satisfying
N_{\text{dir}}\leq 10^{12}
and
N_{\text{dir}}\geq 4c^{2}
.

4.

For the noise variance
\sigma^{2}\geq C_{\text{dim}}d
where
C_{\text{dim}}
is a universal constant, then we can control all the dimension-dependent terms.

5.

Finally, we need that
n\geq C_{\text{num.samples}}
where
C_{\text{num.samples}}
is a universal constant.

6.

For the required dimension, we need to assume that
d\geq C_{\text{dim}}(n+n^{2}\rho^{2})
. It is a benign assumption since it is the minimum dimension such that even informed attacker can succeed.

It is easy to see that these conditions are not contradictory and can be satisfied by many choices of
(k,\sigma)
.

4
Sample-based MIA with Known Covariance

In this section, we consider an easier scenario where the covariance matrix of the data distribution is known to the attacker, but only the mean vector is unknown. The following result provides a lower bound on the number of auxiliary samples required by any sample-based MIA.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
c
be a universal constant. Consider the sample-based MIA setup introduced in
Definition

2.1
. Let
d,n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho>0
be arbitrary constants that satisfy
n\geq 4
,
m\geq 2
, and
d<c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+n^{2}\rho^{2}+\frac{n^{2}}{m}}}\right)
. Then, there is
no

(\mathcal{P}^{\text{id.cov}}_{d},\{\mathcal{A}_{n,\rho}\},n,m)
-sample-based MIA where
\mathcal{A}_{n,\rho}
is the noisy empirical mean defined in
Definition

2.4
and
\mathcal{P}^{\text{id.cov}}_{d}
is the distribution family defined in
Definition

2.3
.

To understand the implications of this result, consider the parameter regime where
d\asymp n+n^{2}\rho^{2}
. In this range, an informed attacker with full knowledge of the data distribution can satisfy the accuracy conditions in
Definition

2.1
(see
Proposition

2.5
). In contrast, a sample-based MIA requires at least
\newmin\{\frac{1}{\rho^{2}},n\}
auxiliary samples to achieve similar performance. Furthermore, this result is tight:
[
DSSUV15
]
showed that when
d\gtrsim n+n^{2}\rho^{2}+\frac{n^{2}}{m}
, there exists a strategy that satisfies the requirements in
Definition

2.1
for the distribution family
\mathcal{P}^{\text{id.cov}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

Knowledge of the covariance matrix significantly reduces the sample complexity of sample-based MIA. For
\rho>1/\sqrt{n}
, comparing
Theorems

1.1
and
1.2
shows that knowing the covariance reduces the required auxiliary samples from
\Omega(n^{2}\rho^{2})
to
O(n)
.

Proof Sketch.

For the lower bound, we follow the framework in
Section

2.3
. We consider the distribution family
\mathcal{P}^{\text{id.cov}}_{d}=\{\mathcal{N}(\mu,\mathbb{I}_{d}):\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
with prior
\pi=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
over
\mu
. The key insight is that conditioning on auxiliary samples yields tractable posterior distributions, allowing us to compute the total variation distance explicitly.

Setup.
Let
(\mu,Z_{0},Z_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})^{\otimes 3}
be independent, and define
\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}
and
\beta=\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{n^{2}}+\rho^{2}}
. The data point and algorithm output under the two hypotheses are:

OUT:

\displaystyle X_{0}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\mu+Z_{0},\quad\hat{\mu}_{0}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\mu+\alpha Z_{1}

IN:

\displaystyle X_{1}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\mu+Z_{0},\quad\hat{\mu}_{1}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\mu+\frac{Z_{0}}{n}+\beta Z_{1}

Key Step.
Notice that under the two hypotheses, the distribution of auxiliary samples are the same. Therefore, by the chain rule for KL divergence
[
PW25
]
,

\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}|\bm{Y}^{m}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}|\bm{Y}^{m}}}}\right)}}\right].

Conditioning on auxiliary samples
\bm{Y}^{m}=\mathbf{y}^{m}
only affects the distribution of
\mu
. By
Lemma

D.2
, the posterior is

\displaystyle\mu|\bm{Y}^{m}=\mathbf{y}^{m}\sim\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\mathbf{y}}^{m},\frac{1}{m+1}\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right)

where
\bar{\mathbf{y}}^{m}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}
.

Let
\tilde{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
be independent of
(Z_{0},Z_{1})
. We can express the conditional distributions as

\displaystyle X_{i}|\bm{Y}^{m}=\mathbf{y}^{m}

\displaystyle~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\mathbf{y}}^{m}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{m+1}}\tilde{Z}+Z_{0}\quad\text{(same for both $i=0,1$)}

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{0}|\bm{Y}^{m}=\mathbf{y}^{m}

\displaystyle~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\mathbf{y}}^{m}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{m+1}}\tilde{Z}+\alpha Z_{1}

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{1}|\bm{Y}^{m}=\mathbf{y}^{m}

\displaystyle~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\mathbf{y}}^{m}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{m+1}}\tilde{Z}+\frac{Z_{0}}{n}+\beta Z_{1}

Since these conditional distributions are Gaussian with explicit covariance structures, we can compute their KL divergence in closed form. The key observation is that the KL divergence scales as
O(d/(n+n^{2}\rho^{2}+n^{2}/m))
. Applying Pinsker’s inequality
[
PW25
]
then yields the desired bound on the total variation distance, completing the proof.
∎

A
Sample Complexity of Mahalanobis Distance Estimation

Definition A.1
.

Fix
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in(0,1]
. We say a (possibly randomized) algorithm
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is an
\alpha
-accurate Mahalanobis norm estimator using
n
samples if the following holds for every unit-norm
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and positive definite
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
: let
\mathbf{X}^{n}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)^{\otimes(n)}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1-\alpha)y^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}y\leq\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})\leq(1+\alpha)y^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}y}}\right)\geq 0.95.

Theorem A.2
.

Let
0<c_{1}<1
and
c_{2}>1
be universal constants. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d>c
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a unit-norm vector. Then, for every
\frac{1}{2}
-accurate Mahalanobis norm estimator for
y
with
n
samples, we require
n\geq c_{1}d
. To achieve this bound using
n>c_{2}d
samples, a
\frac{1}{2}
-accurate Mahalanobis norm estimator for
y
is given by
y^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}y
where
\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}
is the inverse of the empirical covariance using
n
samples.

Proof.

In
Lemma

A.5
, we show that any Mahalanobis norm estimator can be converted into a tester for the following binary hypothesis testing problem:

Definition A.3
.

Fix
d\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Define the following binary hypothesis testing problem:

\displaystyle H_{0}:P_{0}=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{0})~\text{where}~\Sigma_{0}=\mathbb{I}_{d},

\displaystyle H_{1}:\text{sample $v\sim\text{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{d-1})$ then}~P_{v}=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{v})~\text{where}~\Sigma_{v}=\mathbb{I}_{d}-vv^{\top}+\frac{1}{31d}vv^{\top}.

Let
\pi=\text{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{d-1})
. We say a tester
T:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{n}\to\{0,1\}
with sample size
n
has accuracy
0.55
, if

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\sim P_{0}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=1}}\right)\leq 0.45,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{Pr}_{(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\sim P_{v}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=0}}\right)}}\right]\leq 0.45.

Then, in
Lemma

A.6
, we show that any tester requires at least
c_{1}d
samples to achieve a non-trivial accuracy. This completes the proof.

The upper bound follows from the well-known results on the estimation of the covariance matrix in the operator norm
[
Ver18
]
.
∎

A.1
Supporting Lemmas

Lemma A.4
.

Let
d\geq 7
. Fix a unit-norm vector
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
v\sim\text{Unif}(\mathcal{S}_{d-1})
. Then, with probability at least
\frac{3}{5}
, we have
y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y\geq 4
where
\Sigma_{v}=\mathbb{I}_{d}-vv^{\top}+\frac{1}{31d}vv^{\top}
.

Proof.

Notice
\Sigma_{v}^{-1}=\mathbb{I}_{d}+(31d-1)vv^{\top}
. Therefore,

y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|y}}\right\|^{2}+(31d-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v},{y}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}.

Next, we provide a probabilistic lowerbound on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v},{y}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}
. By rotational invariance of
v
, we can assume
y=(1,0,\dots,0)
. Therefore,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v},{y}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~v_{1}^{2}
. Then, we use that
v~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{g}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|g}}\right\|}
where
g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})
, to write

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v},{y}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{10d}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{g_{1}^{2}}{g_{1}^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{d}g_{i}^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{10d}}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(g_{1}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}\wedge\sum_{i=2}^{d}g_{i}^{2}\leq\frac{9}{4}d}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(g_{1}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}}}\right)\cdot\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=2}^{d}g_{i}^{2}\leq\frac{9}{4}d}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 0.60,

where the last step follows from numerical evaluations. Therefore, with probability at least
0.6
, we have

\displaystyle y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|y}}\right\|^{2}+(31d-1)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v},{y}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}\geq 1+(31d-1)\frac{1}{10d}\geq 4.

∎

Lemma A.5
.

Fix a
\frac{1}{2}
-accurate Mahalanobis norm estimator denoted by
\Phi
with sample size
n
(see
Definition

A.1
.). Then, by postprocessing the output of
\Phi
, we can convert
\Phi
to a hypothesis tester that distinguishes
H_{0}
from
H_{1}
with accuracy at least
0.55

Proof.

The tester we propose is defined as follows. The tester first fixes a unit-norm vector
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, its decision rule is given by

\displaystyle T(\mathbf{X}^{n})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})\leq 1.75\\
1&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

First consider the problem under
H_{0}
. Let
\mathbf{X}^{n}\sim P_{0}^{\otimes n}
. Under
H_{0}
, we have
\Sigma_{0}=\mathbb{I}_{d}
. Therefore,
y^{\top}\Sigma_{0}^{-1}y=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|y}}\right\|^{2}=1
. Based on
Definition

A.1
, we have
0.5\leq\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})\leq 1.5
with probability at least
0.95
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{X}^{n}\sim P_{0}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T(\mathbf{X}^{n})=1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{X}^{n}\sim P_{0}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})>1.75}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 0.05

\displaystyle\leq 0.45.

Thus, it satisfies the accuracy condition under
H_{0}
. Next, consider the accuracy under
H_{1}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{X}^{n}\sim P_{v}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(T(\mathbf{X}^{n})=0}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{X}^{n}\sim P_{v}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})\leq 1.75}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{X}^{n}\sim P_{v}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})\leq 1.75}}\right)\big|y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y\geq 4}}\right]\mathrm{Pr}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y\geq 4}}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{X}^{n}\sim P_{v}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})\leq 1.75}}\right)\big|y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y<4}}\right]\mathrm{Pr}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y<4}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{X}^{n}\sim P_{v}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})\leq 1.75}}\right)\big|y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y\geq 4}}\right]+\mathrm{Pr}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y<4}}\right).

From
Lemma

A.4
, we have
\mathrm{Pr}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y\geq 4}}\right)\geq\frac{3}{5}
. Under the event that
\{y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y\geq 4\}
, based on
Definition

A.1
, we have that
2\leq\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})
with probability at least
0.95
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{Pr}_{\mathbf{X}^{n}\sim P_{v}^{\otimes n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi(y,\mathbf{X}^{n})\leq 1.75}}\right)\big|y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y\geq 4}}\right]+\mathrm{Pr}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y^{\top}\Sigma_{v}^{-1}y<4}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{20}+\frac{2}{5}

\displaystyle=0.45,

as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Let
c
be a universal constant and
d\geq 7
be an integer. Recall the testing problem in
Definition

A.3
. Then, any hypothesis tester for distinguishing
H_{0}
from
H_{1}
using
n
samples with an accuracy of
0.55
requires at least
n\geq cd
samples.

Proof.

In this proof, we use a proof technique attributed to Ingster and Suslina
[
IS12
]
. Before delving into details of the proof, we review some properties of chi-squared divergence. For proofs and more discussions see
[
PW25
,
Duc23
]
. For two distributions
P
and
Q
such that
P\ll Q
defined over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with probability density functions (wrt to Lebesgue measure)
p
and
q
, the chi-squared divergence is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\chi^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}Q}}}\right)=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{p^{2}(x)}{q(x)}\text{d}x-1.

Let
P_{0}=\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})
and recall the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{v}}}\right]
from
Definition

A.3
. To show the hardness of testing, our goal is to characterize the total variation distance using
[
Duc23
, Prop. 2.9]
which states

\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{v}^{\otimes n}}}\right]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}P_{0}^{\otimes n}}}}\right)}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{D}_{\chi^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{v}^{\otimes n}}}\right]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}P_{0}^{\otimes n}}}}\right)+1}}\right)}.

(7)

We also need
[
Duc23
, Lem. 13.2.3]
which states

1+\mathrm{D}_{\chi^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{v}^{\otimes n}}}\right]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}P_{0}^{\otimes n}}}}\right)=\mathbb{E}_{(v_{1},v_{2})\sim\pi^{\otimes 2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{p_{v_{1}}(X)p_{v_{2}}(X)}{p_{0}^{2}(X)}}}\right]}}\right)^{n}}}\right].

(8)

In the first step we invoke
Lemma

D.15
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X\sim p_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{p_{v_{1}}(X)p_{v_{2}}(X)}{p_{0}^{2}(X)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{p_{v_{1}}(x)p_{v_{2}}(x)}{p_{0}(x)}\text{d}x

(9)

\displaystyle=(\det(\Sigma_{v_{1}})\det(\Sigma_{v_{2}}))^{-1/2}(\det(\Sigma_{v_{1}}^{-1}+\Sigma_{v_{2}}^{-1}-I))^{-1/2},

Notice that
\det(\Sigma_{v_{1}})=\det(\Sigma_{v_{2}})=\frac{1}{31d}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\Sigma_{v_{1}}^{-1}+\Sigma_{v_{1}}^{-1}-\mathbb{I}_{d}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}_{d}+(31d-1)v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}+\mathbb{I}_{d}+(31d-1)v_{2}v_{2}^{\top}-\mathbb{I}_{d}

(10)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}_{d}+(31d-1)v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}+(31d-1)v_{2}v_{2}^{\top}.

For notational convenience let
\gamma=31d-1
. From
Lemma

D.16
, we have

\det\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{I}_{d}+\gamma v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}+\gamma v_{2}v_{2}^{\top}}}\right)=1+2\gamma+\gamma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}}}\right).

(11)

Thus, plugging
Equations

10
and
11
into
Equation

9
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X\sim p_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{p_{v_{1}}(X)p_{v_{2}}(X)}{p_{0}^{2}(X)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=(\det(\Sigma_{v_{1}})\det(\Sigma_{v_{2}}))^{-1/2}(\det(\Sigma_{v_{1}}^{-1}+\Sigma_{v_{2}}^{-1}-I))^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1+2\gamma+\gamma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma}}\right)^{2}}}}\right)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma}}\right)^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{-1/2}.

Using this result by
Equation

8
, we can write

\displaystyle 1+\mathrm{D}_{\chi^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{v}^{\otimes n}}}\right]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}P_{0}^{\otimes n}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(v_{1},v_{2})\sim\pi^{\otimes 2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{p_{v_{1}}(X)p_{v_{2}}(X)}{p_{0}^{2}(X)}}}\right]}}\right)^{n}}}\right]

(12)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(v_{1},v_{2})\sim\pi^{\otimes 2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma}}\right)^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{-\frac{n}{2}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{(v_{1},v_{2})\sim\pi^{\otimes 2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{-\frac{n}{2}}}}\right].

Consider
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>
. By rotational invariance, we can assume that
v_{2}=[1,0,\cdots,0]^{\top}
. Therefore,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~v_{1}^{(1)}
, i.e., the first element of a uniformly distributed random variable on the unit-sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We use the following well known fact: the distribution of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{1}^{(1)}}}\right)^{2}
follows beta distribution with parameters
\mathsf{beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{d-1}{2}}}\right)

[
Wik24
]
. Then, we use the closed-form expression for the PDF of the beta distribution to write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(v_{1},v_{2})\sim\pi^{\otimes 2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{-\frac{n}{2}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{d-1}{2}}}\right)}\int_{x\in[0,1]}\frac{1}{(1-x)^{\frac{n}{2}}}x^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-x)^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\text{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{d-1}{2}}}\right)}\int_{x\in[0,1]}x^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-x)^{\frac{d-n-3}{2}}\text{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{d-n-1}{2}}}\right)}{\mathrm{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{d-1}{2}}}\right)},

where
\mathrm{B}
is the beta function. We also need to have
d>n+1
so that integral exists. As we will see, this assumption is benign. By the equivalent definition of the beta function in terms of Gamma function,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{d-n-1}{2}}}\right)}{\mathrm{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{d-1}{2}}}\right)}=\frac{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d-n-1}{2}}}\right)\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{2}}}\right)}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d-n}{2}}}\right)\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d-1}{2}}}\right)}.

To control this term, we use the well-known Gautschi’s inequality
[
Gau59
]
which states that for every
x>1
, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{x-1}\leq\frac{\Gamma(x)}{\Gamma(x-\frac{1}{2})}\leq\sqrt{x},

Therefore, using the previous steps, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(v_{1},v_{2})\sim\pi^{\otimes 2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{-\frac{n}{2}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d-n-1}{2}}}\right)\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{2}}}\right)}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d-n}{2}}}\right)\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d-1}{2}}}\right)}

(13)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{d}{d-n-1}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1+\frac{n+1}{d-n-1}}.

Combining all the previous steps, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{v}^{\otimes n}}}\right]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}P_{0}^{\otimes n}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{D}_{\chi^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{v}^{\otimes n}}}\right]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}P_{0}^{\otimes n}}}}\right)+1}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\text{From \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:tv-dist}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}_{(v_{1},v_{2})\sim\pi^{\otimes 2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}}}\right)^{-\frac{n}{2}}}}\right]}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\text{From \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:simplifiy-chi-squared}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{1+\frac{n+1}{d-n-1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\text{From \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:pf-beta}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{n+1}{d-n-1}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{n+1}{d-n-1}}.

\displaystyle(\log(1+x)\leq x)

Therefore, given
d\geq(1+c^{2})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+1}}\right)
for a sufficiently (and universally) large
c
, we have that

\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathbb{E}_{v\sim\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[P_{v}^{\otimes n}}}\right]}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}P_{0}^{\otimes n}}}}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{2c},

as was to be shown.
∎

B
Proofs from
Section

3

B.1
Proof of the Lower Bound

See
3.5

Proof.

Recall from
Definition

2.1
that
\Sigma=\mathbb{I}_{d}+\sigma^{2}UU^{\top}
. Lets consider the following two sets of random variables:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{\|}^{m}=\{UU^{\top}Y_{i}\}_{i\in[m]},\quad,\bm{Y}_{\perp}^{m}=\{(\mathbb{I}_{d}-UU^{\top})Y_{i}\}_{i\in[m]}.

Since
d>2m
and
k>m
, we have that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}_{\|}^{m}}}\right)
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}_{\perp}^{m}}}\right)
are linearly independent vectors with probability one. Therefore
\mathrm{span}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}_{\|}^{m}}}\right)
and
\mathrm{span}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}_{\perp}^{m}}}\right)
are two
m
dimensional subspaces in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let us denote this event by
\mathcal{G}
. In the rest of the argument, we condition on this almost sure event.

Using data processing inequality, we have

\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}\right)}\leq\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},\bm{Y}_{\|}^{m},\bm{Y}_{\perp}^{m}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1},\bm{Y}_{\|}^{m},\bm{Y}_{\perp}^{m}}}}\right)}.

(14)

Define the following three orthogonal projection matrices
(\Pi_{\|},\Pi_{\perp},\Pi_{\text{unk}})
:

•

\Pi_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the projection matrix onto the subspace spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}_{\perp}^{m}}}\right)
,

•

\Pi_{\|}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the orthogonal projection matrix onto the subspace spanned by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}_{\|}^{m}}}\right)
,

•

\Pi_{\text{unk}}=\mathbb{I}_{d}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}+\Pi_{\perp}}}\right)
.

Define the random variable
\mathrm{Aux}=(Y_{\text{p}}^{m},Y_{\text{o}}^{m},\Pi_{\perp},\Pi_{\|},\Pi_{\text{unk}})
. Using the chain rule for TV from
Lemma

D.1
and the fact that for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
\Pi_{\perp}(x)+\Pi_{\|}(x)+\Pi_{\text{unk}}(x)=x
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},\bm{Y}_{\|}^{m},\bm{Y}_{\perp}^{m},\Pi_{\|},\Pi_{\perp},\Pi_{\text{unk}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1},\bm{Y}_{\|}^{m},\bm{Y}_{\perp}^{m},\Pi_{\|},\Pi_{\perp},\Pi_{\text{unk}}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]}_{\text{First Term}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux},\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux},\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)}}}\right)}}}\right]}_{\text{Second term}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux},\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right),\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux},\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right),\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)}}}\right)}}}\right]}_{\text{Third term}}.

(15)

In what follows, we provide an upper bound on each term separately. First, recall
Definition

2.1
and the construction in
Algorithm

1
. Let
(Z_{0},Z_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})^{\otimes 2}
and
(W_{0},W_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{k})^{\otimes 2}
where
(W_{0},W_{1},Z_{0},Z_{1})
are mutually independent. Also, let
\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}
and
\beta=\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{n}+\rho^{2}}
. Then, we can represent the random variables we considered as

\displaystyle X_{0}

\displaystyle=Z_{0}+\sigma UW_{0},\quad\hat{\mu}_{0}=\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{1}+\sigma UW_{1}}}\right).

(16)

\displaystyle X_{1}

\displaystyle=Z_{0}+\sigma UW_{0},\quad\hat{\mu}_{1}=\frac{Z_{0}+\sigma UW_{0}}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{1}+\sigma UW_{1}}}\right).

Also, notice that conditioning on
\mathrm{Aux}
only changes the distribution of
UU^{\top}
6
6

6

Notice that
U
is not unique. However, as we will show, the final result is invariant of the choice of
U
.
.

First Term in
Equation

15
:

First, we claim that
\Pi_{\perp}U=0
. The proof is as follows: let
UU^{\top}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}u_{j}u_{j}^{\top}
where
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}
are orthonormal basis. Then, for every
j\in[k]
and every
i\in[m]
, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{j}},{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{I}_{d}-UU^{\top}}}\right)Y_{i}}}}}}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{j}},{Y_{i}}}}}}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{j}},{UU^{\top}Y_{i}}}}}}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{j}},{Y_{i}}}}}}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{UU^{\top}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(u_{j}}}\right)},{Y_{i}}}}}}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{j}},{Y_{i}}}}}}}}\right>-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{j}},{Y_{i}}}}}}}}\right>=0
where we used the fact that
u_{j}
are in the subspace whose projection matrix is
UU^{\top}
. Therefore, every
u_{j}
is orthogonal to
\mathrm{span}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}_{\perp}^{m}}}\right)}}\right)
. Then, using this observation and
Equation

16

\displaystyle\Pi_{\perp}X_{0}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\perp}Z_{0},\quad\Pi_{\perp}\hat{\mu}_{0}=\alpha\Pi_{\perp}Z_{1}.

(17)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\perp}X_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\perp}Z_{0},\quad\Pi_{\perp}\hat{\mu}_{1}=\frac{\Pi_{\perp}Z_{0}}{n}+\beta\Pi_{\perp}Z_{1}.

Consider an arbitrary eigenvalue decomposition of
\Pi_{\perp}=VV^{\top}
where
V\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
is a matrix with orthonormal columns with probability one. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\perp}Z_{0},\alpha\Pi_{\perp}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\perp}Z_{0},\frac{\Pi_{\perp}Z_{0}}{n}+\beta\Pi_{\perp}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}VV^{\top}Z_{0},\alpha VV^{\top}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}VV^{\top}Z_{0},\frac{VV^{\top}Z_{0}}{n}+\beta VV^{\top}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}V^{\top}Z_{0},\alpha V^{\top}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}V^{\top}Z_{0},\frac{V^{\top}Z_{0}}{n}+\beta V^{\top}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c\sqrt{\frac{m}{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}},

where
(a)
follows from invariance of the total variation distance to one-to-one mappings. Then, notice that
V^{\top}Z_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{m})
,
V^{\top}Z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{m})
, and
V^{\top}Z_{1}\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}V^{\top}Z_{0}
. The last step follows since the total variation in Step
(a)
corresponds to MIA for the full-information setting in dimension
m
. (See
Proposition

2.5
.) Finally, notice that the final bound is invariant to the choice of
V
and
U
.

Second Term in
Equation

15
:

First, from
Equation

16
, we can write

\displaystyle\Pi_{\|}X_{0}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\|}Z_{0}+\sigma\Pi_{\|}UW_{0},\quad\Pi_{\|}\hat{\mu}_{0}=\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}Z_{1}+\sigma\Pi_{\|}UW_{1}}}\right).

(18)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\|}X_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\|}Z_{0}+\sigma\Pi_{\|}UW_{0},\quad\Pi_{\|}\hat{\mu}_{1}=\frac{\Pi_{\|}Z_{0}+\sigma\Pi_{\|}UW_{0}}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}Z_{1}+\sigma\Pi_{\|}UW_{1}}}\right).

Note from
Equation

17
, we have the following Markov chain
UU^{\top}\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Aux}\leftrightarrow\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{j},\hat{\mu}_{j}}}\right)
for
j\in\{0,1\}
. The key observation is that since
\Pi_{\|}
and
\Pi_{\perp}
are the projection matrices onto the orthogonal subspaces, by the properties of Gaussian random variables, we have
\Pi_{\|}Z_{j}\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}\Pi_{\perp}Z_{j}
for
j\in\{0,1\}
. Therefore, from
Equation

17
and
Equation

18
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux},\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux},\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right].

The second key observation is the following: for a
fixed

UU^{\top}
and a
fixed

\Pi_{\|}
, consider the covariance matrix of the Gaussian random variable
\Pi_{\|}Z_{j}+\sigma\Pi_{\|}UW_{j}
for
j\in\{0,1\}
. The covariance matrix is given by
\Pi_{\|}+\sigma^{2}\Pi_{\|}UU^{\top}\Pi_{\|}
. We claim that
\Pi_{\|}+\sigma^{2}\Pi_{\|}UU^{\top}\Pi_{\|}=(1+\sigma^{2})\Pi_{\|}
. The proof is as follows: by construction
\mathrm{Im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}}}\right)\subseteq\mathrm{Im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(UU^{\top}}}\right)
and by definition for every
y\in\mathrm{Im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(UU^{\top}}}\right)
, we have
UU^{\top}y=y
. Thus, for an arbitrary vector
r
, we have
UU^{\top}\Pi_{\|}r=\Pi_{\|}r
. Then, because
\Pi_{\|}
is a projection matrix, we have
\Pi_{\|}^{2}=\Pi_{\|}
. Therefore,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}+\sigma^{2}\Pi_{\|}UU^{\top}\Pi_{\|}}}\right)r=\Pi_{\|}r+\sigma^{2}\Pi_{\|}^{2}r=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\sigma^{2}}}\right)\Pi_{\|}r
. This observation lets us to show the following: Let
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}Z_{j}+\sigma\Pi_{\|}UW_{j}\in B|\text{Aux}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{UU^{\top}|\text{Aux}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}Z_{j}+\sigma\Pi_{\|}UW_{j}\in B|\text{Aux},UU^{\top}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}_{Z_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\Pi_{\|}Z_{j}\in B}}\right).

Based on this result, we can simplify
Equation

18
: Let
(Z_{0},Z_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})^{\otimes 2}
. Then,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\|}X_{0}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\Pi_{\|}Z_{0},\quad\Pi_{\|}\hat{\mu}_{0}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\alpha\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\Pi_{\|}Z_{1}.

(19)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\|}X_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\Pi_{\|}Z_{0},\quad\Pi_{\|}\hat{\mu}_{1}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\Pi_{\|}Z_{0}}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}\Pi_{\|}Z_{1}}}\right).

By the invariance of the total variation distance to one-to-one mappings, we can divide all the random variables by
\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}
to write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\|}Z_{0},\alpha\Pi_{\|}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\|}Z_{0},\frac{\Pi_{\|}Z_{0}}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}Z_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right].

Then, consider an arbitrary eigenvalue decomposition of
\Pi_{\|}=VV^{\top}
where
V\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
is a matrix with orthonormal columns with probability one. Then, notice that
\Pi_{\|}Z_{j}=VV^{\top}Z_{j}
and
V^{\top}Z_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{m})
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\|}Z_{0},\alpha\Pi_{\|}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\|}Z_{0},\frac{\Pi_{\|}Z_{0}}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}Z_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}V^{\top}Z_{0},\alpha V^{\top}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}V^{\top}Z_{0},\frac{V^{\top}Z_{0}}{n}+\beta V^{\top}Z_{1}\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c\sqrt{\frac{m}{n+n^{2}\rho^{2}}},

where the last step follows since the total variation in Step
(a)
corresponds to MIA in the full-information setting in dimension
m
(See
Proposition

2.5
.) Also, notice that the final bound is invariant to the choice of
V
.

Third Term in
Equation

15
:

In the first step, for
i\in\{0,1\}
, we claim that the following Markov chain holds:

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right),\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right)}}\right)\leftrightarrow\mathrm{Aux}\leftrightarrow\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right)

To prove this claim we use
Lemma

D.14
. In particular, using the notation of
Lemma

D.14
, let
A=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right),\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right)}}\right)
,
B=\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right)
,
C=\mathrm{Aux}
, and
M=UU^{\top}
. First of all as we showed in the previous two steps,
\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right),\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right)
are independent of
UU^{\top}
conditioned on
\mathrm{Aux}
. Then, note that conditioned on
UU^{\top}
and
\mathrm{Aux}
,
A
and
B
are jointly Gaussian random variables and for Gaussian random variables, independence is equivalent to zero correlation. Then, using these facts it is straightforward to show that
A\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}B\big|\mathrm{Aux},UU^{\top}
. Therefore, by
Lemma

D.14
, we have the claimed Markov chain. Using this step,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux},\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right),\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux},\Pi_{\|}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right),\Pi_{\perp}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right].

(20)

In the next step, we prove a structural result regarding the conditional distribution of
UU^{\top}
on
\mathrm{Aux}
. Let
V_{1}V_{1}^{\top}=\Pi_{\|}
and
V_{2}V_{2}^{\top}=\Pi_{\text{unk}}
where
V_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
and
V_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-2m)}
are matrices with orthonormal columns with probability one. Also, let
\tilde{U}\tilde{U}^{\top}
be a uniformly random subspace of dimension
k-m
in
\mathbb{R}^{d-2m}
which is independent of all other random variables, therefore,
\tilde{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d-2m)\times(k-m)}
. Then, combining
Lemma

D.12
and
Lemma

D.13
, we have

UU^{\top}\big|\mathrm{Aux}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~V_{1}V_{1}^{\top}+V_{2}\tilde{U}\tilde{U}^{\top}V_{2}^{\top}.

(21)

The proof of this result is as follows: In
Lemma

D.12
, we give a general description of all the projection matrices
\Pi
that satisfies the following two conditions: given a fixed set of vectors
(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{m}
and
(x_{i}=\Pi(y_{i}))_{i\in[m]}
. Then, in
Lemma

D.13
, we show that since we choose the prior as uniform, the conditional distribution is also uniform over all the projection matrices characterized by
Lemma

D.12
.

Using this key result, we represent the random variables in
Equation

20
as follows: let
(\tilde{Z}_{0},\tilde{Z}_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d-2m})^{\otimes 2}
and
(\tilde{W}_{0},\tilde{W}_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{k-m})^{\otimes 2}
where
(\tilde{W}_{0},\tilde{W}_{1},\tilde{Z}_{0},\tilde{Z}_{1})
are mutually independent. Also,
\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}
and
\beta=\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{n}+\rho^{2}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\text{unk}}X_{0}\big|\mathrm{Aux}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~V_{2}\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma V_{2}\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0}

(22)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\text{unk}}\hat{\mu}_{0}\big|\mathrm{Aux}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{2}\tilde{Z}_{1}+\sigma V_{2}\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{1}}}\right).

\displaystyle\Pi_{\text{unk}}X_{1}\big|\mathrm{Aux}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~V_{2}\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma V_{2}\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0}

\displaystyle\Pi_{\text{unk}}\hat{\mu}_{1}\big|\mathrm{Aux}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{V_{2}\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma V_{2}\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0}}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{2}\tilde{Z}_{1}+\sigma V_{2}\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{1}}}\right),

For notational convenience, let
A=\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0}
and
B=\tilde{Z}_{1}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{1}
. Then, we can simplify
Equation

20
as follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\Pi_{\text{unk}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{2}A,\alpha V_{2}B}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{2}A,\frac{V_{2}A}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{2}B}}\right)}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,\alpha B}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,\frac{A}{n}+\beta B}}\right)\Big|\mathrm{Aux}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0},\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{Z}_{1}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{1}}}\right)}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0},\frac{\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0}}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{2}\tilde{Z}_{1}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{1}}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)},

where Step
(a)
follows from the invariance of TV to one-to-one mappings. Notice that the last step can be written as follows: let
(\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}})=\textsc{Sample}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n,d-2m,k-m,0,\sigma}}\right)
, then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0},\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{Z}_{1}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{1}}}\right)}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0},\frac{\tilde{Z}_{0}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{0}}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(V_{2}\tilde{Z}_{1}+\sigma\tilde{U}\tilde{W}_{1}}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\widetilde{X_{0}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\widetilde{X_{1}},\widetilde{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}\right)}.

Thus, combining all the steps concludes the proof.

∎

See
3.6

Proof.

We prove the statement for the case
j=0
, the case of
j=1
follows similarly. By definition, we can write

\displaystyle X_{0}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~Z_{1}+\sigma^{2}UZ_{2},

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{0}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{3}+\sigma^{2}UZ_{4}}}\right).

Here
(Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3},Z_{4})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})^{\otimes 4}
and
\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}
. The main observation is that for every rotation matrix
R
, we have
RZ_{1}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~Z_{1}
,
RZ_{3}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~Z_{3}
, and
RU~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~U
. Therefore, the claim follows as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma B.1
.

Let
(X,Y)
be a pair of random variables in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a joint density given by
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. Assume that for every rotation matrix
R\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X,Y}}\right)~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~(RX,RY)
. Then, there exists a function
g:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for every
x,y\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
,

\displaystyle f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)=g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|y}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{y}}}}}}}\right>}}\right).

Proof.

In the first step, we show that for every rotation matrix
R
and
(x,y)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
we have

f(x,y)=f(Rx,Ry)
. The proof is as follows. Let
(A,B)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((X,Y)\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,B}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{(A,B)}f(x,y)\text{d}x\text{d}y

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((RX,RY)\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A,B}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((X,Y)\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R^{-1}A,R^{-1}B}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{(R^{-1}A,R^{-1}B)}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)\text{d}x\text{d}y

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\int_{(A,B)}f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R^{-1}x,R^{-1}y}}\right)\text{d}x\text{d}y,

where
(a)
follows from the assumption and
(b)
follows from the change of variable and the fact that
\det(R)=1
. Since it holds for every measurable set
(A,B)
, we have
f(Rx,Ry)=f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)
.

Let
(n_{1},n_{2},r)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Consider the following set
C=\{(x,y):(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|y}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{y}}}}}}}\right>)=(n_{1},n_{2},r)\}
. In the final step, we show that
f
is constant over the set
C
. Consider two pairs of points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in C
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in C
. It is immediate to see there exists a rotation matrix
R
such that
(Rx_{1},Ry_{1})=(x_{2},y_{2})
. Therefore,
f(x_{1},y_{1})=f(Rx_{1},Ry_{1})=f(x_{2},y_{2})
. Thus,
f
is constant on
C
and as a result for every
(x,y)\in C
, we have
f(x,y)=g(n_{1},n_{2},r)
, as was to be shown.
∎

See
3.7

Proof.

Let
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
where
T(x,y)=(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|y}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{y}}}}}}}\right>)
. By the data processing inequality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},T(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0})}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1},T(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1})}}}\right)}.

In the next step, using the chain rule for TV from
Lemma

D.1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0},T(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0})}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1},T(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1})}}}\right)}\leq\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}T(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0})}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}T(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1})}}}\right)}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0})\Big|T\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1})\Big|T\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)}}}\right)}}}\right].

We claim the last term is zero. For
j\in\{0,1\}
, conditional on the event that
T(X_{j},\hat{\mu}_{j})=t
, the distribution of
(X_{j},\hat{\mu}_{j})
is uniform over the set
T^{-1}(t)
by
Lemma

3.6
and
Lemma

B.1
. The reason is that as we show in
Lemma

B.1
, for
i\in\{0,1\}
, the density function of
X_{i},\hat{\mu}_{i}
only depends on the triple of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{i}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{i}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{i}},{\hat{\mu}_{i}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)
.
∎

See
3.8

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>,X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>,X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>,X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>,X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)},

where
(a)
follows by the data processing inequality, and
(b)
follows because the norm of a random variable is a deterministic function of it. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>,X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>,X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|\Big|X_{0},UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|\Big|X_{1},UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>}}}\right)}}}\right],

where the last line follows because
\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}=0
.
∎

See
3.9

Proof.

In the first step, we use Pinsker’s inequality
[
PW25
]
and Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}}\right]}.

Consider the following
conditional
KL divergence, where with an abuse of notation we use
UU^{\top}
as the realization of
UU^{\top}
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top}}}}\right).

Conditional on
UU^{\top}
and
x
, by the construction in
Algorithm

1
, we can represent
\hat{\mu}_{0}
and
\hat{\mu}_{1}
as follows: let the subspace spanned by
UU^{\top}
is given by
\mathrm{span}\{\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|},u_{2},\dots,u_{k}\}
,
Z_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d}),(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes k}
such that
(Z_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k})
are mutually independent. Then,

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{0}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}\xi_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right),

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{x}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}\xi_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right),

We have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{u_{i}}}}}}}}\right>=0
for
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. Also,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{UU^{\top}x}}}}}}}\right>=x^{\top}UU^{\top}UU^{\top}x=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\alpha\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sigma\xi_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}}\right)\sim\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\alpha^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}+\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{n}+\beta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sigma\xi_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}}\right)\sim\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{n},\beta^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}+\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right).

Let
\Sigma=\mathbb{I}_{d}+\sigma^{2}UU^{\top}
and notice that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}+\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}=x^{\top}\Sigma x
. By the closed-form expression for the KL divergence between two uni-variate Gaussian distributions, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{2(n+n^{2}\rho^{2})}+\frac{1}{2(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})}+\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{4}}{2(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})x^{\top}\Sigma x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}+\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{4}}{2(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})x^{\top}\Sigma x}.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{0},UU^{\top}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>\Big|X_{1},UU^{\top}}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}+\mathbb{E}_{UU^{\top}\sim\pi,X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d}+\sigma^{2}UU^{\top})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X}}\right\|^{4}}{2(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})X^{\top}\Sigma X}}}\right].

(23)

By definition, we can represent
X=\Sigma^{1/2}Z
where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})
. Then, by the rotational invariance of Gaussian random variables, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{UU^{\top}\sim\pi,X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d}+\sigma^{2}UU^{\top})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X}}\right\|^{4}}{X^{\top}\Sigma X}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z^{\top}\Sigma Z}}\right)^{2}}{Z^{\top}\Sigma^{2}Z}}}\right],

where using the rotation invariance of Gaussian random variable, we can assume

\Sigma=\text{diag}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\underbrace{1+\sigma^{2},\dots,1+\sigma^{2}}_{\text{$k$ times}},\underbrace{1,\dots,1}_{\text{$d-k$ times}}}}\right)

. For notational convenience, let
Z=(Z_{1},Z_{2})
where
Z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{k})
,
Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d-k})
, and
Z_{1}\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}Z_{2}
. Using this and by
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
for every
a,b
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z^{\top}\Sigma Z}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{4}+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{4},

(24)

\displaystyle Z^{\top}\Sigma^{2}Z

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{2}.

Let
\gamma=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\sigma^{2}}}\right)^{2}
. Then, from
Equation

24
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z^{\top}\Sigma Z}}\right)^{2}}{Z^{\top}\Sigma^{2}Z}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z^{\top}\Sigma Z}}\right)^{2}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-sZ^{\top}\Sigma^{2}Z}}\right)\text{d}s}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s=0}^{\infty}2\gamma\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{4}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-s\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right]\text{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{s=0}^{\infty}2\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{4}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-s\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right]\text{d}s.

(25)

We start with the first term in
Equation

25
.

\displaystyle\int_{s=0}^{\infty}2\gamma\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{4}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-s\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right]\text{d}s

(26)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{s=0}^{\infty}2\gamma\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{4}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-s\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right]\text{d}s

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{16\gamma}{(1+2s\gamma)^{\frac{k}{2}+3}}\frac{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}+2}}\right)}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}}}\right)}\text{d}s

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{8\gamma}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+2s\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{k}{2}+3}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k+3}}\right)^{2}\text{d}s

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\frac{8(k+3)^{2}}{k+4}

\displaystyle\leq 8(k+4),

where
(a)
follows from
Lemma

D.6
,
(b)
follows from
Lemma

D.7
, and
(c)
follows from
Lemma

D.5
. For the second term in
Equation

25
,

\displaystyle\int_{s=0}^{\infty}2\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{4}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-s\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right]\text{d}s

(27)

\displaystyle=\int_{s=0}^{\infty}2\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{4}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-s\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{2}}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-s\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right]\text{d}s

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{4(d-k+3)^{2}}{(1+2s)^{(d-k)/2+3}}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{(1+2s\gamma)^{k/2}}}}\right)\text{d}s

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d-k+3}}\right)^{2}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(1+2s\gamma)^{k/2}}\text{d}s

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}4\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d-k+3}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k-2}}\right)},

where
(a)
follows
Lemmas

D.6
and
D.7
and the known bounds on the moment generating function of the chi-squared distribution

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-s\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z_{1}}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right]=\frac{1}{(1+2s\gamma)^{k/2}}
. Then,
(b)
follows from
Lemma

D.5
.

The statement follows by using
Equation

26
and
Equation

27
to show an upper bound on
Equation

25
. Then, by plugging this bound into
Equation

23
the statement follows.
∎

See
3.10

Proof.

For a fixed
x
,
UU^{\top}
, and
r
, consider the following total variation distance

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|\Big|X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>=r}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|\Big|X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}}\right)}.

Define
\Pi_{x}
and
\Pi_{x^{\perp}}
as the projection operator on the subspace spanned by
x
and
x^{\perp}
, i.e., the orthogonal subspace to
x
. For
j\in\{0,1\}
, we can write

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{j}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{j}}}\right)+\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{j}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\hat{\mu}_{j}},{\frac{x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|}}}}}}}}\right>+\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{l}}}\right)

\displaystyle=r\frac{x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}+\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{j}}}\right),

where the last step follows because we condition on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{j}}}}}}}}\right>=r
. For notational convenience, we use

\displaystyle E_{0}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>=r\},

(28)

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq\{X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>=r\}.

(29)

Therefore, by invariance to one-to-one mapping of the total variation, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}

(30)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right)}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right)}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}.

Conditioned on
UU^{\top}
and
x
, by the construction in
Algorithm

1
, we can represent
\hat{\mu}_{0}
and
\hat{\mu}_{1}
as follows: Let the subspace
\mathrm{Im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(UU^{\top}}}\right)
be
\mathrm{span}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{u_{1}\triangleq\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|},u_{2},\dots,u_{k}}}\right\}
where
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}
forms an orthogonal basis and
u_{i}\perp x
for
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. Also,
Z_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d}),(\xi_{1,0},\xi_{1,1},\dots,\xi_{k})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{k+1}
such that
(Z_{0},\xi_{1,0},\xi_{1,1},\dots,\xi_{k})
are mutually independent. Also,
\alpha_{0}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}
and
\alpha_{1}\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{n^{2}}+\rho^{2}}
. Then, we can represent
\hat{\mu}_{0}
and
\hat{\mu}_{1}
as follows

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{0}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\alpha_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}\xi_{1,0}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right),

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\frac{x}{n}+\alpha_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}\xi_{1,1}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right).

Recall the specific basis vectors we pick for
\mathrm{Im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(UU^{\top}}}\right)
and its properties. Then, we decompose
\hat{\mu}_{0}
and
\hat{\mu}_{1}
on the subspace spanned by
x
and the subspace orthogonal to
x
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{0}&=\alpha_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}}}\right)+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}}}\right)\xi_{1,0}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right),\\
\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{1}&=\alpha_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}}}\right)+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}}}\right)\xi_{1,1}+\sum_{i=2}^{k}u_{i}\xi_{i}}}\right)}}\right).\end{cases}

\begin{cases}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>&=\alpha_{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|\xi_{1,0}}}\right),\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>&=\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{n}+\alpha_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|\xi_{1,1}}}\right).\end{cases}

(31)

We make the following two observations: 1)
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}}}\right)
by the properties of the Gaussian distribution. 2) For
j\in\{0,1\}
, conditioning on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{\hat{\mu}_{j}}}}}}}}\right>=r
only changes the distribution of
\xi_{1,j}
.

For notational convenience, let

v\triangleq\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}}}\right)
and observe that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\leq 1
. Recall we assume that the subspace
\mathrm{Im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(UU^{\top}}}\right)
is given by
\mathrm{span}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{u_{1}\triangleq\frac{UU^{\top}x}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|},u_{2},\dots,u_{k}}}\right\}
where
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}\}
forms an orthogonal basis and
u_{i}\perp x
for
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
. Notice that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v},{u_{j}}}}}}}}\right>
for
j\in\{2,\cdots,k\}
is zero. It is because
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v},{u_{j}}}}}}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\Pi_{x^{\perp}}u_{1}},{u_{j}}}}}}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{1}},{\Pi_{x^{\perp}}u_{j}}}}}}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{1}},{u_{j}}}}}}}}\right>=0
where we used that 1)
\Pi_{x^{\perp}}
is a symmetric matrix, 2)
\Pi_{x^{\perp}}u_{j}=u_{j}
for
j\in\{2,\dots,k\}
.

Using this observation, we can represent
\Pi_{x^{\perp}}=HH^{\top}
where
H\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-1)}
, and
H
is given by

H=\begin{bmatrix}v&u_{1}&\cdots&u_{k}&\tilde{u}_{k+1}&\cdots&\tilde{u}_{d-1}\end{bmatrix},

where
\tilde{u}_{k+1},\cdots,\tilde{u}_{d}
is the completion of the orthogonal basis for
\mathrm{Im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Pi_{x^{\perp}}}}\right)
. Therefore,

\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{0}}}\right)=v\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sum_{j=2}^{k}u_{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{j}},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sum_{j=k+1}^{d-1}\tilde{u}_{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\tilde{u}_{j}},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>.

(32)

Note that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{1}},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>,\dots,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{u_{k}},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\tilde{u}_{k+1}},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>,\dots,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\tilde{u}_{d-1}},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes(d-1)}
since
\{v,u_{1},\dots,u_{m}\}
