are orthonormal vectors.

The observation in
Equation

32
implies the following: As conditioning only changes the distribution of
\xi_{1,0}
and
\xi_{1,1}
, by the rotational invariance of the Gaussian distribution we have for
j\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{j}}}\right\|^{2}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\alpha_{j}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,j})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y+W}}\right),

where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
Y\sim\chi^{2}(k-1)
,
W\sim\chi^{2}(d-k-1)
, and
(Z,\xi_{1,0},\xi_{1,1},Y,W)
are mutually independent.

Then, we can write

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,0})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y+W}}\right)\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,1})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y+W}}\right)\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,0})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,1})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)},

where in the last line we decompose
Y=Y_{1}+Y_{2}
where
Y_{1}\sim\chi^{2}((k-1)/2)
,
Y_{2}\sim\chi^{2}((k-1)/2)
, and
Y_{1}\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}Y_{2}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,0})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,1})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)}}}\right)},

(33)

where Step
(a)
follows from the data processing inequality and chain rule. Also, notice that we can drop the conditioning term as
W
is independent of the events
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
.

Next, we provide an upper bound
for
Equation

33
as follows. Let
\alpha_{0}^{2}/\alpha_{1}^{2}=1+\frac{1}{n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}\triangleq 1+\gamma
. Then, by the invariance of the TV distance to one-to-one mapptings,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma}}\right)Y_{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma}}\right)\frac{W}{1+\sigma^{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y_{2}+\frac{W}{1+\sigma^{2}}}}}\right)}

Now, we are in the position to use
Lemma

D.10
to provide an upper bound on this term. First of all notice that we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{(1+\gamma)W}{1+\sigma^{2}}-\frac{W}{1+\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\mathbb{E}[W^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2(d-k-1)+(d-k-1)^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2d+d^{2}}}\right)

where in the last line we used
W\sim\chi^{2}(d-k-1)
. Thus, from
Lemma

D.10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma}}\right)Y_{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\gamma}}\right)\frac{W}{1+\sigma^{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y_{2}+\frac{W}{1+\sigma^{2}}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{2d+d^{2}}}{4}+\frac{\gamma^{2}k}{8}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\frac{2d+d^{2}}{2k-18}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{2d+d^{2}}}{4(1+\sigma^{2})}+\frac{k}{8(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}\frac{2d+d^{2}}{(2k-18)(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}}.

(34)

In the next step, we provide an upper bound on the first term in
Equation

33
. Consider
Equation

28
. We can represent
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
using the representation in
Equation

31
as follows:

\displaystyle E_{0}

\displaystyle=\{X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>=r\}

\displaystyle=\{X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}}}\right\|\xi_{1,0}}}\right)=\frac{r}{\alpha_{0}}\},

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\{X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>=r\}

\displaystyle=\{X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{x},{Z_{0}}}}}}}}\right>+\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}}}\right\|\xi_{1,1}}}\right)=\frac{r-\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{n}\}}{\alpha_{1}}.

From
Lemma

D.8
, we can represent the conditional distribution of
\xi_{1,0}
and
\xi_{1,1}
on
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
as follows: Let
\tilde{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
such that it is independent of other random variables. Then,

\displaystyle\xi_{1,0}\Big|E_{0}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\mu_{0}+h\tilde{Z},

\displaystyle\xi_{1,1}\Big|E_{1}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\mu_{1}+h\tilde{Z},

where

\displaystyle\mu_{0}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{r}{\alpha_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}}}\right),

(35)

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq\frac{r-\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{n}}{\alpha_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}}}\right),

(36)

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}+\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}}}\leq 1.

(37)

Using the closed-form expression for the conditional distribution
\xi_{1,0}
and
\xi_{1,1}
on
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,0}}}\right)^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,1})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{0}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{1}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}.

(38)

Let
c>1
be a constant that will be determined later. Define the following
good
event:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=\Big\{

\displaystyle|r|\leq c\alpha_{0}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}

(39)

\displaystyle\cap\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k-c\sqrt{k}}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k+c\sqrt{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle\cap\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|(I-UU^{\top})x}}\right\|^{2}\leq(d-k)+c\sqrt{d-k}\Big\}.

Under the event
\mathcal{G}
, we have the following deterministic bound on
\mu_{0}
from
Equation

35
:

\displaystyle|\mu_{0}|

\displaystyle=\frac{|r|}{\alpha_{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}}}\right)

(40)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}c,

where
(a)
follows from the assumption that under the event
\mathcal{G}
, we have
|r|\leq c\alpha_{0}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}
.
(b)
follows by the fact that the term inside the parenthesis is less than one.

Under the event
\mathcal{G}
, we have the following deterministic bound on
\mu_{1}
from
Equation

35
:

\displaystyle|\mu_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|r|}{\alpha_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}}}\right)+\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{n\alpha_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\frac{c\alpha_{0}}{\alpha_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}}}}\right)+\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{n\alpha_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c\alpha_{0}}{\alpha_{1}}+\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{n\alpha_{1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}2c+\frac{1}{n\alpha_{1}}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}{\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|}

where
(a)
follows from the assumption that under the event
\mathcal{G}
, we have
|r|\leq c\alpha_{0}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}x}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|x}}\right\|^{2}}
,
(b)
follows from
\alpha_{0}/\alpha_{1}\leq 2
for
n\geq 2
and simple algebraic manipulations. Then,

\displaystyle|\mu_{1}|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}2c+\frac{1}{n\alpha_{1}}\frac{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k+c\sqrt{k}}}\right)+(d-k)+c\sqrt{d-k}}{\sigma\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k-c\sqrt{k}}}\right)}}

(41)

\displaystyle\leq 2c+\frac{1}{n\alpha_{1}}\frac{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k+c\sqrt{k}}}\right)+d+c\sqrt{d}}{\sigma\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k-c\sqrt{k}}}\right)}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}2c+\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{k}}+\sqrt{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{\leq}}2c+\frac{\sqrt{32k}}{\sqrt{n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{\leq}}3c,

where Step
(c)
follows by using the assumption that under the event
\mathcal{G}
, we have a deterministic upper and lower bounds on the norms, and
(d)
follows from given that
d>k\geq 4c^{2}
, we have
k+c\sqrt{k}\leq 2k
,
d+c\sqrt{d}\leq 2d
, and
k-c\sqrt{k}\geq k/2
. Step
(e)
follows from assuming
d\leq\sigma^{2}k
, and Step
(f)
by
32k\leq c^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}}\right)
.

To control the total variation term in
Equation

38
, we aim to use
Lemma

D.10
. Consider,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{0}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Bigg|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{1}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Bigg|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\underbrace{\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{0}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})}}_{R_{0}}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}Y_{1}}}\right)\Bigg|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\underbrace{\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{1}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})}}_{R_{1}}+Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R_{0}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}Y_{1}\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}R_{1}+Y_{1}\Bigg|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R_{0}+(1+\gamma)Y_{1}\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}R_{1}+Y_{1}\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)},

where
\alpha_{0}^{2}/\alpha_{1}^{2}=1+\frac{1}{n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}\triangleq 1+\gamma
. Also, notice that
Y_{1}
is independent of
E_{0}
(and
E_{1}
).

If
(x,UU^{\top},r)\in\mathcal{G}
, where
\mathcal{G}
defined in
Equation

45
, we have an upper bound on
|\mu_{0}|
and
|\mu_{1}|
from
Equation

40
and
Equation

41
. Assume
(x,UU^{\top},r)\in\mathcal{G}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[R^{2}_{0}\Big|E_{0}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{1}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})}}}\right)^{2}\Bigg|X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}\right]

(42)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{1}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}{\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}}}}\right)^{4}\Bigg|X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{8Z^{4}+8\sigma^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8(\mu_{1})^{4}+8h^{4}(\tilde{Z})^{4}}}\right)}{(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}\Bigg|X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{24+64\sigma^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(27c^{4}+3}}\right)}{1+\sigma^{4}}\Bigg|X_{1}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{1}},{\hat{\mu}_{1}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 64\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(27c^{4}+3}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 1792c^{4},

where Step
(a)
follows
(v+w)^{4}\leq 8v^{4}+8w^{4}
holds for every
v,w
, Step
(b)
follows because
|h|\leq 1
,
\mathbb{E}[Z^{4}]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}^{4}]=3
, and
|\mu_{1}|\leq 3c
from
Equation

41
. Also, the last step uses the fact that
c>1
.

Similarly, assume
(x,UU^{\top},r)\in\mathcal{G}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[R^{2}_{1}\Big|E_{1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{0}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})}}}\right)^{2}\Bigg|X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}\right]

(43)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}4\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{0}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)}{\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}}}}\right)^{4}\Bigg|X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}4\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8Z^{4}+8\sigma^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8\mu^{4}_{0}+8h^{4}\tilde{Z}^{4}}}\right)}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{1+\sigma^{2}}}}\right)^{4}}\Bigg|X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}4\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(24+8\sigma^{4}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(8c^{4}+24}}\right)}}\right)}{1+\sigma^{4}}\Bigg|X_{0}=x,UU^{\top}=UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>=r}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 256\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c^{4}+3}}\right),

where Step
(a)
follows because
\alpha_{0}^{2}/\alpha_{1}^{2}\leq 2
, Step
(b)
follows from
(v+w)^{4}\leq 8v^{4}+8w^{4}
holds for every
v,w
, Step
(c)
follows because
|h|\leq 1
,
\mathbb{E}[Z^{4}]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}^{4}]=3
, and
|\mu_{1}|\leq c
from
Equation

40
.

With an slight abuse of notation denote the random variable
\tilde{R}_{i}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~R_{i}|E_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. As we showed in
Equations

42
and
43
, given
(x,UU^{\top},r)\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\tilde{R}_{0}-\tilde{R}_{1}|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tilde{R}_{0}^{2}}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\tilde{R}_{1}^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 1792c^{4}+256c^{4}+768

\displaystyle\leq 2049c^{4},

Then, equipped with this inequality, we use
Lemma

D.10
. Given
(x,UU^{\top},r)\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{0}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}Y}}\right)\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu_{1}+h\tilde{Z}}}\right)}}\right)^{2}}{(1+\sigma^{2})}+Y}}\right)\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R_{0}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}Y\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}R_{1}+Y\Bigg|E_{1}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{12c^{2}}{n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}+\frac{k}{8(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}+\frac{1024c^{4}}{k-9}}.

(44)

The last step is providing an upper bound on the probability of event
\mathcal{G}^{c}
. By an application of union bound, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((X_{0},UU^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>)\in\mathcal{G}^{c}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{X_{0}},{\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}}}}}\right>>c\alpha_{0}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}X_{0}}}\right\|^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|X_{0}}}\right\|^{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k-c\sqrt{k}}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|UU^{\top}X_{0}}}\right\|^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k+c\sqrt{k}}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|(I-UU^{\top})X_{0}}}\right\|^{2}\leq(d-k)+c\sqrt{d-k}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{c^{2}},

(45)

where the last step follows by Chebyshev’s inequality. Putting everything together, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Pi_{x^{\perp}}\hat{\mu}_{1}}}\right\|^{2}\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,0})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,1})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}}}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,0})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((Z+\sigma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|v}}\right\|\xi_{1,1})^{2}+(1+\sigma^{2})Y_{1}}}\right)\Big|E_{1}}}}\right)}\mathds{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{G}}}\right]}}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{G}^{c}}}\right)+\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\alpha_{0}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\alpha_{1}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((1+\sigma^{2})Y_{2}+W}}\right)}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\frac{12c^{2}}{n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2}}+\frac{k}{8(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}+\frac{1024c^{4}}{k-9}}+\frac{3}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{1}{(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}\frac{\sqrt{2d+d^{2}}}{4(1+\sigma^{2})}+\frac{k}{8(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(n-1+n^{2}\rho^{2})^{2}}\frac{2d+d^{2}}{(2k-18)(1+\sigma^{2})^{2}}}.

Here
(a)
follows from
Equation

30
,
(b)
follows from the fact that TV is less than one, and
(c)
follows from
Equations

34
,
44
and
45
.
∎

B.2
Proof of the Upper Bound

See
3.3

Proof.

We give here the sketch of the proof as the proof is very similar to
[
DSSUV15
]
. We show a simple reduction that shows that by having
m=\Omega(d)
samples, we can reduce the problem to the sample-based MIA with identity covariance.

Let the unknown data distribution be
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
. Fix
c>1
to be a constant that will be determined later. Let
m=c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d+\newmin\{n,\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\}}}\right)+1=m_{1}+m_{2}+1
. Since
d\geq\Omega(n+n^{2}\rho^{2})
, we have
m=\Theta(d)
.
Let
H=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{m_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}Y_{i}Y_{i}^{\top}}}\right)^{-1}
,
\bar{\mu}_{0}=\frac{1}{m_{2}}\sum_{i=m_{1}+1}^{m_{2}+m_{1}}
. Given
m_{1}\geq cd
,
H
exists with probability one. Consider the following test statistics

\displaystyle\psi_{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{Y}^{m},\hat{\mu},X}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{H^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)},{H^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right)}}}}}}}\right>.

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{H^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)},{H^{1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right)}}}}}}}\right>=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma^{1/2}H\Sigma^{1/2}-\mathbb{I}_{d}+\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right)\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right).

From
Lemma

D.3
, given
m_{1}\geq cd
for sufficiently large
c
, with probability at least
0.99
,

-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{I}_{d}\preceq\Sigma^{1/2}H\Sigma^{1/2}-\mathbb{I}_{d}\preceq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{I}_{d}.

Therefore, with probability at least
0.99
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma^{1/2}H\Sigma^{1/2}-\mathbb{I}_{d}+\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right)\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right),

(46)

and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma^{1/2}H\Sigma^{1/2}-\mathbb{I}_{d}+\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right)\Sigma^{-1/2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right).

(47)

Now conditioned on the event that
Equation

46
and
Equation

47
hold, we claim that we can analyze
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right)
by assuming that the covariance is identity. We have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\mu}-\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X-Y_{0}}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma^{-1/2}\hat{\mu}-\Sigma^{-1/2}\bar{\mu}_{0}}}\right)^{\top}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma^{-1/2}X-\Sigma^{-1/2}Y_{0}}}\right).

Notice that marginally
\Sigma^{-1/2}X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\mathbb{I}_{d})
,
\Sigma^{-1/2}Y_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\mathbb{I}_{d})
, and
\Sigma^{-1/2}\bar{\mu}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\mathbb{I}_{d}/m_{2})
. Finally, we claim that
\Sigma^{-1/2}\hat{\mu}
is a member of
\mathfrak{A}_{n,\rho,d}
for the distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\mathbb{I}_{d})
given
\hat{\mu}
is a member of
\mathfrak{A}_{n,\rho,d}
for
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
. The last claim is an immediate observation from the definition in
Definition

3.2
. Thus, the problem can be reduced to the known covariance case. The rest of the proof follows from
[
DSSUV15
]
.

∎

C
Proofs from
Section

4

See
4.1

Proof.

Recall the definition of the family of the data distributions in
\mathcal{P}^{\text{id.cov}}_{d}
. Fix
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and define
Z_{0},Z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})
where
Z_{0}\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}Z_{1}
,
\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}
, and
\beta=\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{n^{2}}+\rho^{2}}
. Then, consider the following (correlated) random variables

\displaystyle X_{0}=\mu+Z_{0},\quad\hat{\mu}_{0}=\mu+\alpha Z_{1},

\displaystyle X_{1}=\mu+Z_{0},\quad\hat{\mu}_{1}=\mu+\frac{Z_{0}}{n}+\beta Z_{1}.

Define the distribution
\pi=\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})
. We aim to use
Section

2.3
to prove a lower bound and in particular we assume that
\mu\sim\pi
. Using
Section

2.3
and the chain rule for the KL divergence, we have

\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}|\bm{Y}^{m}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}|\bm{Y}^{m}}}}\right)}}\right].

(48)

Consider
\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}|\bm{Y}^{m}=\bm{y}^{m}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}|\bm{Y}^{m}=\bm{y}^{m}}}}\right)
. Notice that conditioning on
\bm{Y}^{m}=\bm{y}^{m}
(auxiliary samples) only changes the distribution of
\mu
. In particular, using
Lemma

D.2
, we have

\displaystyle\mu|\bm{Y}^{m}=\bm{y}^{m}\sim\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\bm{y}}^{m},\frac{1}{m+1}\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right),

where

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{y}}^{m}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}.

Let
(\tilde{Z},Z_{0},Z_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})^{\otimes 3}
. Using this observation, we can represent the random variables that we compare their KL divergence in
Equation

48
as follows:

\displaystyle X_{0}=\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\bm{y}}^{m}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+m}}\tilde{Z}+Z_{0},\quad\hat{\mu}_{0}=\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\bm{y}}^{m}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+m}}\tilde{Z}+\alpha Z_{1},

\displaystyle X_{1}=\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\bm{y}}^{m}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+m}}\tilde{Z}+Z_{0},\quad\hat{\mu}_{1}=\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\bm{y}}^{m}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+m}}\tilde{Z}+\frac{Z_{0}}{n}+\beta Z_{1},

By the invariance property of the KL divergence to the translation of the random variables, we can subtract all the random variables by
\frac{m}{m+1}\bar{\bm{y}}^{m}
. Therefore,
Equation

48
is given by

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}|\bm{Y}^{m}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}|\bm{Y}^{m}}}}\right)}}\right]=\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\Sigma_{1}}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\Sigma_{2}}}\right)}}}\right),

(49)

where
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are

\Sigma_{2}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\frac{m+2}{m+1}\mathbb{I}_{d}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{m+1}\mathbb{I}_{d}\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{m+1}\mathbb{I}_{d}&\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{m+1}+\alpha^{2}}}\right)\mathbb{I}_{d}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\Sigma_{1}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\frac{m+2}{m+1}\mathbb{I}_{d}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{m+1}+\frac{1}{n}\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{m+1}+\frac{1}{n}}}\right)\mathbb{I}_{d}&\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{m+1}+\alpha^{2}}}\right)\mathbb{I}_{d}\end{pmatrix}.

In the next step, we use the closed-form expression for the KL divergence between two Guassians.

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\Sigma_{1}}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\Sigma_{2}}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\frac{\det(\Sigma_{2})}{\det(\Sigma_{1})}-2d+\mathrm{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\Sigma_{1}}}\right)}}\right].

By simple algebraic manipulations,

\displaystyle\det(\Sigma_{2})

\displaystyle=d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{m+1}+\alpha^{2}\frac{m+2}{m+1}}}\right)

\displaystyle\det(\Sigma_{1})

\displaystyle=d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{m+1}+\alpha^{2}\frac{m+2}{m+1}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}-\frac{2}{n(m+1)}}}\right)

\displaystyle-2+\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\Sigma_{1})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{n\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\alpha^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)}}\right)}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\Sigma_{1}}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\Sigma_{2}}}\right)}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{m+1+2n}{n^{2}+\alpha^{2}n^{2}(m+2)-(m+1)-2n}}}\right)-\frac{2}{n\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\alpha^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right].

Using
\log(1+x)\leq x
, we can provide an upper bound on the KL divergence as follows

\displaystyle\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{m+1+2n}{n^{2}+\alpha^{2}n^{2}(m+2)-(m+1)-2n}}}\right)-\frac{2}{n\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\alpha^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m+1+2n}{n^{2}+n^{2}\alpha^{2}(m+2)-(m+1)-2n}-\frac{2}{n\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\alpha^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)}}\right)}.

Define
r=1+\alpha^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{m+1+2n}{n^{2}+n^{2}\alpha^{2}(m+2)-(m+1)-2n}-\frac{2}{n\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\alpha^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2n+m+1}{n^{2}r-(m+1)-2n}-\frac{2}{nr}

\displaystyle=\frac{mnr+nr-2m-2-4n}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{2}r-(m+1)-2n}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(nr}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{mnr+nr}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n^{2}r-(m+1)-2n}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(nr}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{m+1}{n^{2}r-(m+1)-2n}.

We can provide a lower bound on the denominator as follows

\displaystyle n^{2}r-(m+1)-2n

\displaystyle=n^{2}+n(m+2)+n^{2}\rho^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)-(m+1)-2n

\displaystyle\geq n^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-1}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)+n^{2}\rho^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)-2n

\displaystyle\geq n^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-2}}\right)m+n^{2}\rho^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right),

where in the last step we assume
m\geq 2
. Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle\frac{m+1}{n^{2}r-(m+1)-2n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{m+1}{n^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-2}}\right)m+n^{2}\rho^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+2}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\frac{n^{2}}{m+1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n-2}}\right)\frac{m}{m+1}+n^{2}\rho^{2}\frac{m+2}{m+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\frac{n^{2}}{m+1}+\frac{n}{2}+n^{2}\rho^{2}},

where in the last line we assumed
n\geq 4
. Thus,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\Sigma_{1}}}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\Sigma_{2}}}\right)}}}\right)\leq\frac{d}{\frac{n^{2}}{2(m+1)}+\frac{n}{4}+\frac{n^{2}\rho^{2}}{2}}.

Then, by Pinsker’s inequality
[
PW25
]
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{1},\hat{\mu}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\bm{Y}^{m},X_{0},\hat{\mu}_{0}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{d}{\frac{n^{2}}{4(m+1)}+\frac{n}{8}+\frac{n^{2}\rho^{2}}{4}}}.

By setting the upper bound to a sufficiently small constant, the claim follows.
∎

D
Helper Lemmas

Lemma D.1

(
[
Wu24
]
)
.

Let
(X_{1},Y_{1})
and
(X_{2},Y_{2})
be two pairs of jointly distributed random variables. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{1},Y_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{2},Y_{2}}}}\right)}\leq\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{2}}}}\right)}+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}Y_{1}\Big|X_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y_{2}\Big|X_{2}}}}\right)}}}\right].

Lemma D.2

(
[
Mur07
]
)
.

Fix
m,d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider the following sampling procedure:
\mu\sim\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\sigma^{2}\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right)
,
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}}}\right)\sim\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu,\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right)^{\otimes m}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mu\Big|Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}~\mathrel{\overset{\smash{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny dist.}}}}{=}}~\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{m\sigma^{2}}{m\sigma^{2}+1}\bar{Y}_{m},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{m\sigma^{2}+1}\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right),

where

\displaystyle\bar{Y}_{m}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}Y_{i}.

Lemma D.3

(
[
Ver18
, Thm. 4.7.1]
)
.

Let
c
be a universal constant and
n,d\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})^{\otimes d}
be
n
i.i.d. Gaussian random variables. Then, for every
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}Z_{i}^{\top}-\mathbb{I}_{d}}}\right\|_{op}\leq c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{\frac{d+t}{n}}+\frac{d+t}{n}}}\right)}}\right)\geq 1-2\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-t}}\right)

Lemma D.4
.

Let
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Then, for every
s>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-sX^{2}}}\right)}}\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+2s}}.

Proof.

By the definition,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-sX^{2}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-sx^{2}}}\right)\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}\right)\text{d}x

\displaystyle=\sigma\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\int\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}}\right)\text{d}x

\displaystyle=\sigma,

where
\sigma^{2}=\frac{1}{1+2s}
.
∎

Lemma D.5
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta>1
. Then,

\displaystyle\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(1+\alpha s)^{\beta}}\text{d}s=\frac{1}{\alpha(\beta-1)}.

Proof.

We use the substitution
u=1+\alpha s
, so
du=\alpha ds
and
s=(u-1)/\alpha
. When
s=0
, we have
u=1
, and as
s\to\infty
, we have
u\to\infty
. Thus:

\displaystyle\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(1+\alpha s)^{\beta}}ds

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{u^{\beta}}\cdot\frac{1}{\alpha}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{1}^{\infty}u^{-\beta}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{u^{-\beta+1}}{-\beta+1}}}\right]_{1}^{\infty}.

Since
\beta>1
, we have
-\beta+1<0
, so
u^{-\beta+1}\to 0
as
u\to\infty
. Therefore:

\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(1+\alpha s)^{\beta}}ds=\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{0-1^{-\beta+1}}{-\beta+1}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{-1}{-\beta+1}=\frac{1}{\alpha(\beta-1)}.

∎

Lemma D.6
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
s>0
. Let
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z}}\right\|^{4}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-s\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|Z}}\right\|^{2}}}\right)}}\right]=\frac{8}{(1+2s)^{(\frac{d}{2}+3)}}\frac{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{2}+2}}\right)}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{2}}}\right)}

Proof.

Since
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, we have
\|Z\|^{2}\sim\chi^{2}(d)
with PDF

f_{Y}(y)=\frac{1}{2^{d/2}\Gamma(d/2)}y^{d/2-1}e^{-y/2},\quad y>0.

Let
Y=\|Z\|^{2}
. We compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|Z\|^{4}\exp(-s\|Z\|^{2})}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Y^{2}e^{-sY}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{2}e^{-sy}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{d/2}\Gamma(d/2)}y^{d/2-1}e^{-y/2}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d/2}\Gamma(d/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{(d/2+2)-1}e^{-y(s+1/2)}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d/2}\Gamma(d/2)}\cdot\frac{\Gamma(d/2+2)}{(s+1/2)^{d/2+2}}.

Using
\Gamma(d/2+2)=(d/2+1)(d/2)\Gamma(d/2)
and
s+1/2=(1+2s)/2
:

\displaystyle=\frac{(d/2+1)(d/2)}{2^{d/2}(s+1/2)^{d/2+2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(d/2+1)(d/2)}{2^{d/2}}\cdot\frac{2^{d/2+2}}{(1+2s)^{d/2+2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4(d/2+1)(d/2)}{(1+2s)^{d/2+2}}.

Since
(d/2+1)(d/2)=d(d+2)/4=2\Gamma(d/2+2)/\Gamma(d/2)
and
(1+2s)^{d/2+2}=(1+2s)^{(d/2+3)}
:

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|Z\|^{4}\exp(-s\|Z\|^{2})}}\right]=\frac{8\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{2}+2}}\right)}{(1+2s)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{2}+3}}\right)}\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{2}}}\right)}.

∎

Lemma D.7
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be an integer such that
m\geq 3
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{m}{2}+2}}\right)}{\Gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{m}{2}}}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+3}}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

We start with the fundamental recurrence relation of the Gamma function, which is
\Gamma(z+1)=z\Gamma(z)
. Let
x=\frac{m}{2}
. The LHS of the inequality can be written as
\frac{\Gamma(x+2)}{\Gamma(x)}
. We can expand the numerator using the recurrence relation twice:

\displaystyle\Gamma(x+2)=(x+1)\Gamma(x+1)=(x+1)x\Gamma(x)

Now, we can substitute this back into the LHS expression and simplify:

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(x+2)}{\Gamma(x)}=\frac{(x+1)x\Gamma(x)}{\Gamma(x)}=x(x+1)

Substitute
x=\frac{m}{2}
back into the simplified expression:

\displaystyle x(x+1)=\frac{m}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{m}{2}+1}}\right)=\frac{m}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{m+2}{2}}}\right)=\frac{m(m+2)}{4}=\frac{m^{2}+2m}{4}

Finally, it is obvious that for
m\geq 3
,
\frac{m^{2}+2m}{4}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(m+3}}\right)^{2}
.
∎

Lemma D.8
.

Let
a,b,c
are constants. Let
(X,Y)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes 2}
. Then,

\displaystyle Y\Big|aX+bY=c\sim\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(c\cdot\frac{b}{a^{2}+b^{2}},\frac{a^{2}}{a^{2}+b^{2}}}}\right).

Proof.

Since
(X,Y)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes 2}
, we have
X
and
Y
are independent standard normal random variables.

The linear combination
aX+bY
is normally distributed with

\mathbb{E}[aX+bY]=a\mathbb{E}[X]+b\mathbb{E}[Y]=0.

The variance is

\text{Var}(aX+bY)=a^{2}\text{Var}(X)+b^{2}\text{Var}(Y)=a^{2}+b^{2}.

Therefore
aX+bY\sim\mathcal{N}(0,a^{2}+b^{2})
. Conditioning on
aX+bY=c
gives

Y\mid aX+bY=c\sim\mathcal{N}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{bc}{a^{2}+b^{2}},\frac{a^{2}}{a^{2}+b^{2}}}}\right).

This follows from the standard formula for conditional distributions of jointly normal random variables, where

\mathbb{E}[Y\mid aX+bY=c]=\text{Cov}(Y,aX+bY)\cdot\frac{c}{\text{Var}(aX+bY)}=\frac{b}{a^{2}+b^{2}}\cdot c

and

\text{Var}(Y\mid aX+bY=c)=\text{Var}(Y)-\frac{[\text{Cov}(Y,aX+bY)]^{2}}{\text{Var}(aX+bY)}=1-\frac{b^{2}}{a^{2}+b^{2}}=\frac{a^{2}}{a^{2}+b^{2}}.

∎

Lemma D.9
.

Let
a\geq 0
be a constant and
r\geq 0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k>4
. Let
Y\sim\chi^{2}(k)
. Then,

\displaystyle\newmax\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}a+Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}(1+r)Y}}}\right),\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}a+(1+r)Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}Y}}}\right)}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{a^{2}}{2k-8}+\frac{kr^{2}}{4}+\frac{ar}{2}.

Proof.

We first start with computing the KL divergence between
\gamma Y+a
and
Y
. The KL divergence is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\gamma Y+a\|Y)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\gamma Y+a}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\frac{f_{\gamma Y+a}(X)}{f_{Y}(X)}}}\right].

The chi-squared density is
f_{Y}(y)=\frac{1}{2^{k/2}\Gamma(k/2)}y^{k/2-1}e^{-y/2}
for
y>0
. For
X=\gamma Y+a
, the density is obtained by change of variables:

\displaystyle f_{\gamma Y+a}(x)=\frac{1}{\gamma}f_{Y}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{x-a}{\gamma}}}\right)=\frac{1}{\gamma}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{k/2}\Gamma(k/2)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{x-a}{\gamma}}}\right)^{k/2-1}e^{-\frac{x-a}{2\gamma}},\quad x>a

Using the substitution
X=\gamma Y+a
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\gamma Y+a\|Y)

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{\infty}f_{\gamma Y+a}(x)\log\frac{f_{\gamma Y+a}(x)}{f_{Y}(x)}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{Y}(y)\log\frac{f_{\gamma Y+a}(\gamma y+a)}{f_{Y}(\gamma y+a)}\,dy.

We have

\displaystyle\log\frac{f_{\gamma Y+a}(\gamma y+a)}{f_{Y}(\gamma y+a)}

\displaystyle=\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\frac{1}{\gamma}\cdot y^{k/2-1}e^{-y/2}}{(\gamma y+a)^{k/2-1}e^{-(\gamma y+a)/2}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=-\log\gamma+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}-1}}\right)\log\frac{y}{\gamma y+a}-\frac{y}{2}+\frac{\gamma y+a}{2}

\displaystyle=-\log\gamma+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}-1}}\right)\log\frac{y}{\gamma y+a}+\frac{(\gamma-1)y+a}{2}

Taking expectation with respect to
Y\sim\chi^{2}_{k}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\gamma Y+a\|Y)

\displaystyle=-\log\gamma+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}-1}}\right)\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\frac{Y}{\gamma Y+a}}}\right]+\frac{\gamma-1}{2}\mathbb{E}[Y]+\frac{a}{2}

\displaystyle=-\log\gamma+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}-1}}\right)\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\frac{Y}{\gamma Y+a}}}\right]+\frac{(\gamma-1)k}{2}+\frac{a}{2}

Also, we have

\displaystyle\log\frac{Y}{\gamma Y+a}=-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma+\frac{a}{Y}}}\right)=-\log\gamma-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{a}{\gamma Y}}}\right)

Using the inequality
-\log(1+u)\leq-u+\frac{u^{2}}{2}
for
u>0
:

\displaystyle-\log\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{a}{\gamma Y}}}\right)\leq-\frac{a}{\gamma Y}+\frac{a^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}Y^{2}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\log\frac{Y}{\gamma Y+a}}}\right]\leq-\log\gamma-\frac{a}{\gamma}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{Y}}}\right]+\frac{a^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{Y^{2}}}}\right].

For
Y\sim\chi^{2}_{k}
, we have
\mathbb{E}[1/Y]=\frac{1}{k-2}
for
k>2
and
\mathbb{E}[1/Y^{2}]=\frac{1}{(k-2)(k-4)}
for
k>4
.

Setting
\gamma=1+r
and using
-\log(1+r)\leq-r+\frac{r^{2}}{2}
for
r\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\gamma Y+a\|Y)

\displaystyle\leq-\log(1+r)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{2}-1}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[-\log(1+r)-\frac{a}{(1+r)(k-2)}+\frac{a^{2}}{2(1+r)^{2}(k-2)(k-4)}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{rk}{2}+\frac{a}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{r^{2}k}{4}+\frac{a^{2}}{4k-16}+\frac{ar}{2}.

The proof for
\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}a+Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\big\|}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}(1+r)Y}}}\right)
follows similarly and we skip for brevity.
∎

Lemma D.10
.

Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
be a constant and
k>4
be an integer. Let
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be two random variables with a finite second moment. Let

\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
denote the (marginal) distributions of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, respectively. Fix an arbitrary coupling
\pi\in\Pi(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and draw
(X_{1},X_{2})\sim\pi
. Let
Y\sim\chi^{2}(k)
be independent of
(X_{1},X_{2})
. Define

c^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{(X_{1},X_{2})\sim\pi}\big[(X_{1}-X_{2})^{2}\big].

Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{1}+(1+\gamma)Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{2}+Y}}}\right)}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\gamma c}{4}+\frac{\gamma^{2}k}{4}+\frac{c^{2}}{4k-16}}.

Proof.

Let
\beta=1+\gamma
. By the variational representation of TV
[
PW25
]
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{1}+\beta Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{2}+Y}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\newsup_{f\in[-1,1]}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\big|f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1}+\beta Y}}\right)-f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{2}+Y}}\right)\big|}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\newsup_{f\in[-1,1]}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\big|f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1}+\beta Y}}\right)-f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{2}+Y}}\right)\big|\big|X_{1},X_{2}}}\right]}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{2}\newsup_{f\in[-1,1]}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\big|f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{1}+\beta Y}}\right)-f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{2}+Y}}\right)\big|\big|X_{1},X_{2}}}\right]}}\right],

where the last line follows by interchanging the expectation and
\newsup
. Consider the inner expectation for a fixed values for
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
. Recall that
(X_{1},X_{2})\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}Y
. Therefore, conditioning does not change the distribution of
Y
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\newsup_{f\in[-1,1]}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\big|f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1}+\beta Y}}\right)-f\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{2}+Y}}\right)\big|\big|X_{1}=x_{1},X_{2}=x_{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}x_{1}+\beta Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y+x_{2}}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}x_{1}-x_{2}+\beta Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y}}}\right)}&x_{1}>x_{2}\\
\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\beta Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}x_{2}-x_{1}+Y}}}\right)}&x_{1}\leq x_{2}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq\newmax\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}|x_{1}-x_{2}|+\beta Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y}}}\right)},\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}|x_{1}-x_{2}|+Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\beta Y}}}\right)}}}\right\},

where we used the definition and translation invariance property of TV.

The next step is Pinsker’s inequality
[
PW25
]
,

\displaystyle\newmax\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}|x_{1}-x_{2}|+\beta Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}Y}}}\right)},\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}|x_{1}-x_{2}|+Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}\beta Y}}}\right)}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\newmax\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}|x_{1}-x_{2}|+\beta Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}Y}}}\right)},\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{KL}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}|x_{1}-x_{2}|+Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0.4,0}\beta Y}}}\right)}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|\gamma}{4}+\frac{\gamma^{2}k}{4}+\frac{|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}{4k-16}},

where the last line follows from
Lemma

D.9
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}X_{1}+\beta Y}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\Big\|$}\,}{\color[rgb]{0.7,0.35,0}X_{2}+Y}}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sqrt{\frac{|X_{1}-X_{2}|\gamma}{4}+\frac{\gamma^{2}k}{4}+\frac{|X_{1}-X_{2}|^{2}}{4k-16}}}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}-X_{2}|]\gamma}{4}+\frac{\gamma^{2}k}{4}+\frac{\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}-X_{2}|^{2}]}{4k-16}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}-X_{2}|^{2}]}\gamma}{4}+\frac{\gamma^{2}k}{4}+\frac{\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}-X_{2}|^{2}]}{4k-16}},

where
(a)
follows from Jensen’s inequality, and
(b)
follows from
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|X_{1}-X_{2}|}}\right]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|X_{1}-X_{2}|^{2}}}\right]}
by another application of Jensen’s inequality.
∎

Lemma D.11
.

Fix
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho>0
, and
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. Let
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
be a Gaussian distribution with covariance
\Sigma
.
(X_{0},X_{1},\dots,X_{n},Z)\sim\mathcal{D}^{\otimes(n+2)}
. Let
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}+Z
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\text{Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\hat{\mu}},{X_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma}}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\hat{\mu}},{X_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right]=0
due to the independence of
\hat{\mu}
and
X_{0}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\text{Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\hat{\mu}},{X_{0}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{\hat{\mu}},{X_{0}}}}}}}}\right>^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\mu}^{\top}X_{0}X_{0}^{\top}\hat{\mu}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\mu}^{\top}\Sigma\hat{\mu}_{0}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma\hat{\mu}\hat{\mu}^{\top}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}}\right)\mathrm{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Sigma^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}+\rho^{2}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Sigma}}\right\|_{F}^{2}.

∎

Lemma D.12
.

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be a set of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
be another set of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
n<d
. Let
m
be an integer satisfying
n<m<d/2
. An orthogonal projection operator
\Pi
onto an
m
-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies
\Pi x_{i}=y_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
if and only if two conditions are met:

1.

The subspaces
\mathcal{Y}=\mathrm{span}\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}
and
\mathcal{Z}=\mathrm{span}\{x_{1}-y_{1},\dots,x_{n}-y_{n}\}
are orthogonal, i.e.,
\mathcal{Y}\perp\mathcal{Z}
.

2.

The operator
\Pi
can be expressed as:

\Pi=V_{1}V_{1}^{T}+V_{2}UU^{T}V_{2}^{T}

where:

•

r=\dim(\mathcal{Y})
and
s=\dim(\mathcal{Z})
.

•

V_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
is a matrix whose columns form an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{Y}
.

•

V_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-r-s)}
is a matrix whose columns form an orthonormal basis for the subspace
(\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{Z})^{\perp}
.

•

U\in\mathbb{R}^{(d-r-s)\times(m-r)}
is any matrix with orthonormal columns.

Here
\oplus
denotes the direct sum of subspaces. For subspaces
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the direct sum is
\mathcal{U}\oplus\mathcal{V}=\{u+v:u\in\mathcal{U},v\in\mathcal{V}\},
Also,
⟂
denotes the orthogonal complement. For a subspace
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the orthogonal complement is:

\mathcal{S}^{\perp}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\langle v,s\rangle=0\text{ for all }s\in\mathcal{S}\}

Proof.

(
\implies
)
Necessity.
Assume there exists an orthogonal projection operator
\Pi
onto an
m
-dimensional subspace, which we denote
V=\mathrm{range}(\Pi)
, such that
\Pi x_{i}=y_{i}
for all
i
.

First, we prove the orthogonality condition. Since
\Pi
is a projection and
\Pi x_{i}=y_{i}
, it follows that
\Pi y_{i}=\Pi(\Pi x_{i})=\Pi^{2}x_{i}=\Pi x_{i}=y_{i}
. This implies that
y_{i}\in V
for all
i
, and thus the entire subspace
\mathcal{Y}
is contained in the range of
\Pi
, i.e.,
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq V
.

For an orthogonal projection, the residual vector
x_{i}-\Pi x_{i}
must be orthogonal to the range of the projection. Let
z_{i}=x_{i}-y_{i}=x_{i}-\Pi x_{i}
. Then
z_{i}\in V^{\perp}
for all
i
, which implies that the entire subspace
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq V^{\perp}
. Since
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq V
and
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq V^{\perp}
, the subspaces must be orthogonal, proving the first condition.

Now, we derive the form of
\Pi
. Since
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq V
, the space
V
can be decomposed into the direct sum
V=\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{S}
, where
\mathcal{S}
is the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{Y}
within
V
. The dimension of this complementary subspace is
\dim(\mathcal{S})=\dim(V)-\dim(\mathcal{Y})=m-r
.

As established,
\mathcal{Z}\perp V
, which means any vector in
\mathcal{S}\subseteq V
must be orthogonal to any vector in
\mathcal{Z}
. Furthermore, any vector in
\mathcal{S}
is by definition orthogonal to any vector in
\mathcal{Y}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{S}
must be a subspace of
(\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{Z})^{\perp}
.

The projection operator onto
V
can be written as the sum of projections onto the orthogonal subspaces
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{S}
:
\Pi=\mathrm{proj}_{\mathcal{Y}}+\mathrm{proj}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
The projection onto
\mathcal{Y}
is given by
\mathrm{proj}_{\mathcal{Y}}=V_{1}V_{1}^{T}
.
Since
\mathcal{S}
is an
(m-r)
-dimensional subspace of
(\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{Z})^{\perp}
, we can construct an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{S}
by selecting
m-r
basis vectors from the space spanned by the columns of
V_{2}
. This selection is precisely what the matrix
U
accomplishes. The columns of the matrix
V_{2}U
form an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{S}
. The projection onto
\mathcal{S}
is therefore
\mathrm{proj}_{\mathcal{S}}=(V_{2}U)(V_{2}U)^{T}=V_{2}UU^{T}V_{2}^{T}
.

Combining these gives the required form:
\Pi=V_{1}V_{1}^{T}+V_{2}UU^{T}V_{2}^{T}
.

(
\Longleftarrow
)
Sufficiency.
Assume
\mathcal{Y}\perp\mathcal{Z}
and let
\Pi
be an operator of the given form for some matrix
U
with orthonormal columns. We must show that
\Pi
is an
m
-dimensional orthogonal projection satisfying
\Pi x_{i}=y_{i}
.

Let
P_{1}=V_{1}V_{1}^{T}
and
P_{2}=V_{2}UU^{T}V_{2}^{T}
. The ranges of these operators are
\mathcal{Y}
and a subspace
\mathcal{S}\subseteq(\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{Z})^{\perp}
, respectively. Since these ranges are orthogonal, the projectors are also orthogonal (
P_{1}P_{2}=P_{2}P_{1}=0
).

1.

Projection Property:

\Pi^{2}=(P_{1}+P_{2})^{2}=P_{1}^{2}+P_{2}^{2}+P_{1}P_{2}+P_{2}P_{1}=P_{1}+P_{2}=\Pi
. Thus,
\Pi
is a projection. It is orthogonal because its matrix representation is symmetric.

2.

Dimensionality:
The rank of
\Pi
is the dimension of its range,
\mathcal{Y}\oplus\mathcal{S}
.

\mathrm{rank}(\Pi)=\dim(\mathcal{Y})+\dim(\mathcal{S})=r+(m-r)=m
.

3.

Constraint Satisfaction:
For any
i
, we verify
\Pi x_{i}=y_{i}
. Note that
x_{i}=y_{i}+z_{i}
.

\Pi x_{i}=(P_{1}+P_{2})(y_{i}+z_{i})=P_{1}y_{i}+P_{1}z_{i}+P_{2}y_{i}+P_{2}z_{i}
.

•

P_{1}y_{i}=y_{i}
, since
y_{i}\in\mathrm{range}(P_{1})=\mathcal{Y}
.

•

P_{1}z_{i}=0
, since
z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}\perp\mathcal{Y}
.

•

P_{2}y_{i}=0
, since
y_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathrm{range}(P_{2})\perp\mathcal{Y}
.

•

P_{2}z_{i}=0
, since
z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathrm{range}(P_{2})\perp\mathcal{Z}
.

Combining these results yields
\Pi x_{i}=y_{i}
.

This confirms that any operator of the specified form is a valid projection, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma D.13
.

Let
d,m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
0<m<d
and
n\geq 1
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{d,m}
denote the set of all projection matrices onto a
m
-dimensional subspaces in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
\Pi
denote a random orthogonal projection matrix onto a subspace drawn uniformly from
\mathcal{G}_{d,m}
.

Given:

•

X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}+\sigma^{2}\Pi)
for some
\sigma>0

•

Y_{i}=\Pi X_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n

Then the posterior distribution of
\Pi
given both the observations and their projections is:

\displaystyle d\mathbb{P}(\Pi\mid X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)}{\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\nu(\Pi^{\prime})}d\nu(\Pi)

where
\nu
is the uniform measure on
\mathcal{G}_{d,m}
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\{\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{d,m}:\Pi^{\prime}X_{i}=Y_{i}\text{ for all }i\in[n]\}.

In other words, the posterior is uniform over
\mathcal{M}
with respect to the measure inherited from
\mathcal{G}_{d,m}
.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma=I_{d}+\sigma^{2}\Pi
. Since
\Pi
is an orthogonal projection of rank
m
, it has eigenvalues 1 (with multiplicity
m
) and 0 (with multiplicity
d-m
). Therefore, the eigenvalues of
\Sigma
are
1+\sigma^{2}
(with multiplicity
m
) and 1 (with multiplicity
d-m
).

\displaystyle\det(\Sigma)=(1+\sigma^{2})^{m}\cdot 1^{d-m}=(1+\sigma^{2})^{m}

We use the Sherman-Morrison formula to show that

\displaystyle\Sigma^{-1}=I_{d}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\Pi

Each
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
, so its probability density function is

\displaystyle P(X_{i}\mid\Pi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}\det(\Sigma)^{1/2}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}X_{i}^{T}\Sigma^{-1}X_{i}}}\right)

Substituting
\det(\Sigma)
and
\Sigma^{-1}
:

\displaystyle X_{i}^{T}\Sigma^{-1}X_{i}

\displaystyle=X_{i}^{T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(I_{d}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\Pi}}\right)X_{i}

\displaystyle=X_{i}^{T}X_{i}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}X_{i}^{T}\Pi X_{i}

\displaystyle=\|X_{i}\|^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\|\Pi X_{i}\|^{2}

The joint likelihood for
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
is:

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}P(X_{i}\mid\Pi)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}(1+\sigma^{2})^{m/2}}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\|X_{i}\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\|\Pi X_{i}\|^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}(1+\sigma^{2})^{m/2}}}}\right)^{n}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|X_{i}\|^{2}}}\right)\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\Pi X_{i}\|^{2}}}\right)

For the purpose of finding the posterior distribution of
\Pi
, we can consider terms independent of
\Pi
as proportionality constants. Thus,

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}P(X_{i}\mid\Pi)\propto\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\Pi X_{i}\|^{2}}}\right)

By Bayes’ theorem,

\displaystyle d\mathbb{P}(\Pi\mid X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})\propto P(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\mid\Pi)d\mathbb{P}(\Pi)

The prior
d\mathbb{P}(\Pi)
is the uniform measure
d\nu(\Pi)
on
\mathcal{G}_{d,m}
. The term
P(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\mid\Pi)
can be written as:

P(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\mid X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},\Pi)\cdot P(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\mid\Pi)

Since
Y_{i}
is defined as
\Pi X_{i}
, the term
P(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\mid X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},\Pi)
acts as an indicator function: it is 1 if
Y_{i}=\Pi X_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
, and 0 otherwise. So, incorporating this constraint into the likelihood

\displaystyle d\mathbb{P}(\Pi\mid X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})

\displaystyle\propto\mathbf{1}\{Y_{i}=\Pi X_{i}\text{ for all }i\}\cdot\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\Pi X_{i}\|^{2}}}\right)d\nu(\Pi)

In other words, for any
\Pi
such that
\mathbf{1}\{Y_{i}=\Pi X_{i}\text{ for all }i\}
is non-zero, it must be that
\Pi X_{i}=Y_{i}
. Therefore, for such
\Pi
, we have
\|\Pi X_{i}\|^{2}=\|Y_{i}\|^{2}
. Since
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
are observed and fixed, the term
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Y_{i}\|^{2}
is a constant given the observations. Thus, the exponential term
\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2(1+\sigma^{2})}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Y_{i}\|^{2}}}\right)
is a positive constant and can be absorbed into the overall proportionality constant.

\displaystyle d\mathbb{P}(\Pi\mid X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})\propto\mathbf{1}\{Y_{i}=\Pi X_{i}\text{ for all }i\}d\nu(\Pi)

This proportionality implies that the posterior distribution is uniform over the set
\mathcal{M}=\{\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{m}:\Pi^{\prime}X_{i}=Y_{i}\text{ for all }i\in[n]\}
with respect to the measure
\nu
. Normalizing this distribution yields the stated result.
∎

Lemma D.14
.

Let
(A,B,C,M)
be jointly distributed random variables such that
A\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}B\big|C,M
and
A\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}M\big|C
. Then, we have
A\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}B\big|C
.

Proof.

To illustrate the core logic, we present the proof for the discrete case, which avoids the notational complexity of the general case. Fix
(a,b,c)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A=a,B=b\big|C=c}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A=a,B=b\big|C=c,M=m}}\right)\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M=m\big|C=c}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{m}\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A=a\big|C=c,M=m}}\right)\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B=b\big|C=c,M=m}}\right)\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M=m\big|C=c}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{m}\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A=a\big|C=c}}\right)\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B=b\big|C=c,M=m}}\right)\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M=m\big|C=c}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A=a\big|C=c}}\right)\sum_{m}\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B=b\big|C=c,M=m}}\right)\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M=m\big|C=c}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A=a\big|C=c}}\right)\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B=b\big|C=c}}\right),

where
(a)
follows from
A\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}B\big|C,M
, (b) follows from
A\mathrel{\perp\mkern-9.0mu\perp}M\big|C
, and (c) follows from the law of total probability.
∎

Lemma D.15
.

Let
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}
be positive definite symmetric matrices such that
\Sigma_{1}^{-1}+\Sigma_{2}^{-1}-\mathbb{I}_{d}
be a positive definite symmetric matrix. Let
p_{0}(x),p_{1}(x),p_{2}(x)
be the probability density functions for
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})
,
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{1})
and
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{2})
. Then,

\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{p_{1}(x)p_{2}(x)}{p_{0}(x)}\text{d}x=(\det(\Sigma_{1})\det(\Sigma_{2}))^{-1/2}(\det(\Sigma_{1}^{-1}+\Sigma_{2}^{-1}-I))^{-1/2}

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int\frac{p_{1}(x)p_{2}(x)}{p_{0}(x)}\text{d}x

\displaystyle=\int\frac{(2\pi)^{-d/2}(\det(\Sigma_{1}))^{-1/2}\exp(-x^{T}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}x/2)(2\pi)^{-d/2}(\det(\Sigma_{2}))^{-1/2}\exp(-x^{T}\Sigma_{2}^{-1}x/2)}{(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(-x^{T}x/2)}\text{d}x

\displaystyle=(\det(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}))^{-1/2}\int(2\pi)^{-d/2}\exp(-x^{T}(\Sigma_{1}^{-1}+\Sigma_{2}^{-1}-I)x/2)\text{d}x

\displaystyle=(\det(\Sigma_{1}\Sigma_{2}))^{-1/2}(\det(\Sigma_{1}^{-1}+\Sigma_{2}^{-1}-I))^{-1/2},

where in the last step we used the definition of Gaussian distribution. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.16
.

Fix
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
be two unit norm vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\gamma
be a positive constant. Then,

\displaystyle\det\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{I}_{d}+\gamma v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}+\gamma v_{2}v_{2}^{\top}}}\right)=1+2\gamma+\gamma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left<{{{{{v_{1}},{v_{2}}}}}}}}\right>}}\right)^{2}}}\right).

Proof.

Consider
d\times 2
matrix
V=[v_{1},v_{2}]
. The argument of the determinant is
\mathbb{I}_{d}+\gamma VV^{\top}
. By Sylvester’s determinant identity, we can reduce the problem’s dimension:

\displaystyle\det\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{I}_{d}+\gamma v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}+\gamma v_{2}v_{2}^{\top}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{I}_{d}+\gamma VV^{\top}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{I}_{2}+\gamma V^{\top}V}}\right)

(by Sylvester’s identity)

\displaystyle=\det\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}+\gamma\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}}}\right\rangle\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}}}\right\rangle&1\end{pmatrix}}}\right)

(since
\|v_{1}\|=\|v_{2}\|=1
)

\displaystyle=\det\begin{pmatrix}1+\gamma&\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}}}\right\rangle\\
\gamma\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}}}\right\rangle&1+\gamma\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=(1+\gamma)^{2}-\gamma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}}}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=1+2\gamma+\gamma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle v_{1},v_{2}}}\right\rangle^{2}}}\right),

as was to be shown.
∎
